INTRODUCTION 
1

Our names are Joseph J. Hally and Mark S. Sclafani. 
3

4 
A. 
I am employed by Central Hudson Gas & Electric Corporation (“Central 
5 
Hudson” or the “Company”) and my business address is 284 South 
6

7

8

10

11 
and Forecasts, Energy Policy and Regulation, and Regulatory 
12 
Compliance.  
13

14

15

16

17

18

19

20 
increasing responsibility including Director – Treasury Services, Director 
21

22 
- Energy Transformation & Solutions, and Manager - Regulatory Affairs,

10 
II. 
PURPOSE OF TESTIMONY 
11 
Q.    
What is the purpose of the Panel’s testimony in these proceedings? 
12 
A. 
The Panel’s testimony provides an overview of Central Hudson’s electric 
13 
and gas rate filing.  The filing is consistent with and furthers the 
14 
environmental goals of the Climate Leadership and Community Protection 
15 
Act (“CLCPA”) and it is aligned with New York State’s goals to support 
16 
customers located within a Disadvantaged Community (“DAC”).  The 
17 
Panel’s testimony includes the drivers that have made filing a Rate Case 
18 
at this time necessary and the steps that Central Hudson has undertaken 
19 
to mitigate customer bill impacts.  The Panel also discusses current 
20 
external factors that have occurred in the aftermath of the COVID-19 
21 
Pandemic, including increases in inflation, interest rates, utility arrears, 
22 
supply prices, and employment trends.  Next, The Panel addresses the 
23 
3280


--- Page 19 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE POLICY PANEL 
4 
 
status of the Company’s Management Audit Implementation Plan.  Finally, 
1 
the Panel concludes by introducing Central Hudson’s other panels and 
2 
witnesses.           
3 
Q.    
Is the Policy Panel sponsoring any exhibits in support of its testimony? 
4 
A. 
No. 
5 
III. 
OVERVIEW OF RATE FILING 
6 
Q.    
Please provide a brief overview of Central Hudson’s rate filing. 
7 
A. 
Central Hudson is driven to meet its obligation to provide essential gas 
8 
and electric services in a safe and reliable manner.  It is also vital that 
9 
Central Hudson recover the costs it incurs to continue combating climate 
10 
change by effectively responding to major storms and extreme weather 
11 
events and working towards achieving the critically important and 
12 
ambitious goals of the CLCPA.  This rate filing reflects Central Hudson’s 
13 
core obligation to provide safe and reliable service at just and reasonable 
14 
rates and puts forth a plan identifying the initiatives and investments that 
15 
Central Hudson will need to accomplish within the Rate Year ending June 
16 
30, 2025 (“Rate Year”) to meet this obligation.  Additionally, this rate filing 
17 
continues to devote significant Company resources, time, and attention to 
18 
the pursuit and facilitation of the CLCPA’s goals.  These efforts are 
19 
consistent with achieving New York’s 2030 greenhouse gas emissions 
20 
(“GHG”) emissions reduction target as discussed in the direct testimony 
21 
and exhibits of the Climate Leadership and Sustainability Panel.   
22 
3281


--- Page 20 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE POLICY PANEL 
5 
 
Q.    
What are the external factors that are impacting the Company and its rate 
1 
filing?   
2 
A. 
Central Hudson’s rate filing is being made in the aftermath of the COVID-
3 
19 Pandemic and the Company is experiencing associated increases in 
4 
arrears balances, changes in employee expectations and behaviors, 
5 
significant inflationary pressures, supply price increases, increases in 
6 
interest rates, along with increased pressure on utility credit ratings and 
7 
financial integrity.  All of these factors impact the Company’s rate filing.    
8 
Q.    
Please discuss how the COVID-19 Pandemic has impacted the 
9 
Company’s arrears balances. 
10 
A. 
As discussed further in the direct testimony of the Customer Experience 
11 
Panel, customer arrears balances have grown significantly since the 
12 
Company suspended collections activity for all customers in March of 
13 
2020.  The suspension of collection activities continued until very recently 
14 
in light of the COVID-19 Pandemic, the Parker-Mosley Act of 2020, the 
15 
Parker-Richardson Act of 2021, and collection limitations included within 
16 
the Arrears Reduction Programs.  Additionally, since March 2020, market 
17 
driven electric and gas commodity prices have increased by more than 
18 
100%.  These factors have contributed to significant growth in Central 
19 
Hudson’s arrears balances.  From February 2020 through June 30, 2023, 
20 
residential arrears balances greater than 60 days grew by 1,044% to $96 
21 
million while non-residential arrears grew by 1,971% to $29 million.  
22 
Additionally, the number of residential customers with arrears greater than 
23 
3282


--- Page 21 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____

IV. 
SAP CUSTOMER INFORMATION SYSTEM TRANSITION 
1 
Q.    
Will the Panel please describe the status of the Company’s SAP Customer 
2 
Information System (“CIS”) transition?  
3 
A. 
On September 1, 2021, Central Hudson launched its new SAP CIS.  
4 
Immediately after the launch, there were challenges with the new SAP CIS 
5 
that resulted in impacts to a portion of customers, including delayed and 
6 
inaccurate bills.  Despite its best efforts to correct these issues, Central 
7 
Hudson experienced a rise in customer complaint levels, harsh criticism 
8 
from the media and politicians, and questions from the Commission and 
9 
others seeking to understand the problems with the SAP CIS.  Central 
10 
Hudson has incurred significant incremental expense and expanded its 
11 
efforts with the help of its union and management employees, third-party 
12 
experts, and contractors to correct the SAP CIS transition issues.  To 
13 
measure progress and increase transparency with stakeholders, the 
14 
Company developed a dashboard which tracks Central Hudson’s progress 
15 
on six key metrics.  This dashboard and the six metrics are included within 
16 
Exhibit __ (CEP-1) of the Customer Experience Panel.  Importantly, 
17 
significant improvement can be seen in all of the metrics relative to the 
18 
peak of the SAP CIS issues and the metrics are close to levels associated 
19 
with the period prior to the SAP CIS launch.  Additionally, the Company is 
20 
currently working on all outstanding issues and expects to meet or exceed 
21 
baseline performance levels for each of the six key metrics prior to July 1, 
22 
2024.    
23 
3286

V. 
COST DRIVERS 
1 
Q.    
Please briefly describe the major categories of cost that are driving the 
2 
need for the Company to request rate relief at this time. 
3 
A. 
Despite Central Hudson’s ongoing efforts to mitigate bill impacts, rate 
4 
relief is necessary due to numerous cost drivers.  More specifically, the 
5 
top six drivers, accounting for more than 80% of the electric and gas 
6 
increases are:  1) infrastructure investment driven by the replacement of 
7 
aging or obsolete infrastructure; 2) workforce expansion, attraction, and 
8 
retention; 3) capitalization costs; 4) recovery of major storm expense; 5) 
9 
recovery of Energy Efficiency Programs and New York State Clean Heat 
10 
Program (“Clean Heat Program”) costs; and 6) recovery of Low Income 
11 
Bill Discount Program Expenses.   
12 
Q.    
Please describe why Central Hudson’s electric infrastructure investment is 
13 
a main driver of the electric rate request.  
14 
A. 
The capital program and the associated increase in net plant is driven by 
15 
the need to replace aging or obsolete electric and common infrastructure.  
16 
Replacement of electric transmission and distribution infrastructure is 
17 
required due to the underlying age and condition of the assets and 
18 
accounts for approximately 75% of the planned Electric Five-Year Capital 
19 
Plan expenditures.  In fact, approximately 20% of the Company’s in-
20 
service electric infrastructure assets are beyond their expected useful life.  
21 
The primary benefit of this Five-Year Capital Plan and the replacement of 
22 
infrastructure is to maintain system integrity and customer reliability; 
23 
3288


--- Page 27 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE POLICY PANEL 
12 
 
additionally, the Five-Year Capital Plan also serves to significantly 
1 
increase the capacity to host additional Distributed Energy Resources 
2 
(“DER”) in support of CLCPA targets.  This additional hosting capacity is 
3 
significant and, over the five-year forecast, represents an increase of 547 
4 
MW or two times Central Hudson’s currently interconnected DER 
5 
renewable nameplate capacity.  Investments in electric infrastructure are 
6 
discussed in much greater detail in the direct testimony of the Electric 
7 
Capital and Operations Panel.    
8 
Q.    
Is Central Hudson’s gas infrastructure investment similarly driven by 
9 
infrastructure replacement?   
10 
A. 
Yes.  Over the five-year period approximately 62% of the expenditures 
11 
within the Gas Five-Year Capital Plan are dedicated to replacing aging or 
12 
obsolete equipment.  The majority of this spending is dedicated toward the 
13 
continued elimination of Leak Prone Pipe (“LPP”), which is an essential 
14 
factor in enhancing the safe delivery of natural gas throughout the 
15 
Company’s service territory.  The continued elimination of LPP at a level 
16 
of 15 miles per year (the amount required in the 2021 Rate Plan) is the 
17 
primary cost driver in the gas business.  The direct testimony of both the 
18 
Gas Capital and Operations Panel and the Gas Safety Panel discuss the 
19 
Company’s LPP program and other gas capital expenditures in further 
20 
detail.  Finally, the direct testimony of the Technology Capital and 
21 
Operations Panel discusses the value of IT infrastructure investments and 
22 
how those investments support customer experience optimization, 
23 
3289

cybersecurity, infrastructure hardening and resiliency, and enhance the 
1 
efficiency of business processes.  These common capital expenditures are 
2 
allocated according to the Company’s common allocation of 80% and 20% 
3 
to electric and gas, respectively. 
4 
Q.

5

6

7

8

9

11

16

17 
above normal inflation rates.  These inflationary cost increases have been 
18 
factored into Central Hudson’s Capital Plan.

source of potential moderation is the net regulatory liability of $22 million 
7 
for electric and $12 million for gas.  Another additional source of potential 
8 
moderation for electric customers is approximately $32 million associated 
9 
with an electric rate base credit that originated from the sale of the 
10 
Danskammer and Roseton generating plants and Central Hudson’s 
11 
interest in the Nine Mile Point No. 2 generating plant.  As discussed in the 
12 
direct testimony of the Accounting and Tax Panel, the third potential 
13 
source of rate moderation is utilizing the over funding within the 
14 
Company’s VEBA to decrease medical expense by an estimated $5 
15 
million.  The fourth potential source of moderation could be realized by 
16 
basing the Environmental Site Investigation and Remediation (“SIR”) rate 
17 
allowance on a forecast of spend in the Rate Year instead of the 
18 
traditional methodology, which uses an average of the prior three-year 
19 
actual spend through the Test Year as the basis for determining the 
20 
revenue requirement.  This change would result in a combined change to 
21 
the electric and gas revenue requirements of $4 million.  Within the 
22 
context of multi-year settlement negotiations, Central Hudson is open to 
23 
3299


--- Page 38 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE POLICY PANEL 
23 
 
discussions regarding additional moderation that can be utilized to reduce 
1 
the bill impact to customers.  
2 
VII. 
ADVANCING CLIMATE AND ENERGY LEADERSHIP 
3 
Q.    
Please discuss the ambitious goals set forth in the CLCPA. 
4 
A. 
The CLCPA is the nation-leading legislation that provides specific goals 
5 
for New York’s clean energy transition.  The CLCPA mandates substantial 
6 
reductions in GHG across all sectors of the State’s economy and 
7 
accelerated deployment of clean energy technologies.  There are many 
8 
critical milestones established within the CLCPA, including the reduction 
9 
of economy-wide GHG by 40 percent by 2030, 70% of electricity 
10 
generated by renewable sources by 2030, 100% of electricity generated 
11 
by emissions free sources by 2040, and an 85% reduction in statewide 
12 
GHG by 2050.  Utilities are part of this effort via implementation of 
13 
initiatives aimed at reducing GHG emissions to achieve these State goals.  
14 
In addition, the CLCPA states that, to the extent practicable, spending on 
15 
clean energy and energy efficiency programs, projects, or investments 
16 
shall be directed in a manner that ensures that DACs receive at least 35 
17 
percent, with the goal of 40 percent, of such spending.   
18 
Q.    
How does Central Hudson support the CLCPA?  
19 
A. 
As discussed in the direct testimony of the Climate Leadership and 
20 
Sustainability Panel, Central Hudson has worked to support and facilitate 
21 
the accomplishment of the goals of the CLCPA, while also taking steps to 
22 
3300


--- Page 39 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE POLICY PANEL 
24 
 
enhance corporate focus on sustainability and incorporate climate change 
1 
considerations into its electric and gas operations.   
2 
Q.    
Has Central Hudson included new initiatives within this rate filing that 
3 
support the CLCPA through the reduction of GHG emissions? 
4 
A. 
Yes, within this rate filing, Central Hudson has included significant 
5 
initiatives including major ongoing projects within the Capital Plan such as 
6 
the CLCPA Phase 1 projects and Leak Prone Pipe removal, the 
7 
interconnection of DERs, and existing customer facing programs such as 
8 
the Clean Heat Program and energy efficiency programs.  Additionally, 
9 
Central Hudson is proposing new initiatives that are further described 
10 
within the direct testimonies of the Climate Leadership and Sustainability 
11 
Panel and the Forecasting and Rates Panel.  The following are new 
12 
proposals within this rate filing are consistent with achieving the CLCPA’s 
13 
clean energy goals and GHG targets: 
14 
 the purchase of Responsibly Sourced Gas (“RSG” or “Certified Gas”) 
15 
reduces GHG emissions at the well head, which is the primary source 
16 
of natural gas GHG emissions; 
17 
 the assessment of potential sites for on-site clean hydrogen 
18 
production which may support decarbonization of sectors of the 
19 
economy or specific customers that are not viable candidates for 
20 
electrification;  
21 
 EV Charging Site Assessment Service Program for Multi-Unit 
22 
Dwellings which aims to remove barriers to electrification by providing 
23 
3301


--- Page 40 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE POLICY PANEL 
25 
 
technical assistance to Multi-Unit Dwellings in order to provide access 
1 
to residents seeking access to low-cost EV charging at or near home; 
2 
 EV Education and Outreach Initiative to educate the public and 
3 
customers about the benefits of transportation electrification, EV and 
4 
EV charging options available in the market, and the availability of 
5 
incentives; 
6 
 Onsite Solar generation will allow Central Hudson to offset its own 
7 
electricity use at Company-owned facilities and provide a strong 
8 
message for customers considering similar projects at their home or 
9 
business; 
10 
 Finally, the Company is proposing changes to continue moving 
11 
towards fully eliminating gas declining block rates and eliminate the 
12 
high volume usage discount offering for non-residential gas S.C. No. 6 
13 
customers in order to improve and simplify the delivery rate price 
14 
signal and align it with the energy conservation and emission 
15 
reduction goals of the CLCPA.   
16 
Q.    
Are Central Hudson’s programs aligned with achieving the GHG 
17 
emissions target within the CLCPA? 
18 
A. 
Yes, the Company is steadfast in its support of the economically efficient 
19 
attainment of the CLCPA goals.  As discussed in the direct testimony of 
20 
the Climate Leadership and Sustainability Panel, the Company has 
21 
facilitated a number of initiatives to support the CLCPA and has 
22 
commissioned a third party to review the progress made in relation to the 
23 
3302


--- Page 41 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE POLICY PANEL 
26 
 
CLCPA’s 2030 GHG emissions reduction target.  These initiatives have 
1 
provided a solid foundation supportive of the 2030 GHG emissions 
2 
reduction target, although success is largely dependent on the 
3 
development of large scale renewable generation.   
4 
Q.    
Are Central Hudson’s programs also aligned with providing a significant 
5 
portion of the achieved benefits to customers residing within a DAC?  
6 
A. 
Yes, as discussed by the Climate Leadership and Sustainability Panel, the 
7 
Company estimates that approximately 48% of the GHG benefits 
8 
associated with the Company’s programs would accrue to customers 
9 
within DACs.

VIII. 
EAMS AND PERFORMANCE ADJUSTMENTS 
11 
Q.    
Are the Company’s proposed Earnings Adjustment Mechanisms (“EAMs”) 
12 
aligned with the goals in the CLCPA?  
13 
A. 
Yes.  EAMs are designed to incentivize utilities to facilitate specific 
14 
outcomes that are deemed beneficial to customers and align with State 
15 
policy and legislative goals.  As further described in the direct testimony of 
16 
the Earnings Adjustment Mechanisms Panel, the Company proposes 60 
17 
basis points for electric EAM opportunities and 25 basis points for gas 
18 
EAM opportunities.  These EAM opportunities are allocated among the 
19 
following six EAM measures: 1) Electric and Gas Disadvantaged 
20 
Communities Energy Efficiency Benefits; 2) Electric and Gas Peak 
21 
Reduction; 3) Distributed Energy Resource Utilization; 4) Direct Current 
22 
Fast Charger Electric Vehicle Supply Equipment and Infrastructure 
23 
3303


--- Page 42 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE POLICY PANEL 
27 
 
(“EVSE&I”), 5) Level 2 Charger EVSE&I, and 6) Electric Vehicle Adoption.  
1 
The Company also proposes three tracking only scorecard metrics, which 
2 
include a load factor metric, a residential energy intensity metric, and 
3 
commercial energy intensity metric for reporting purposes only. 
4 
Q.    
Is Central Hudson proposing any changes to its current performance 
5 
adjustment measures? 
6 
A. 
Yes.  Central Hudson is proposing changes to certain performance 
7 
adjustment measures and these changes are discussed further within the 
8 
direct testimonies of the Electric Capital and Operations Panel, the 
9 
Customer Experience Panel, and the Gas Safety Panel.  The Company is 
10 
not proposing a change in the target levels for SAIFI or CAIDI or the basis 
11 
points of potential Negative Revenue Adjustments (“NRA”) at this time but 
12 
notes that the ability for the Company to achieve the proposed levels of 
13 
reliability performance is dependent on the Company’s receiving the 
14 
requested Vegetation Management Program funding levels and the 
15 
funding levels necessary for execution of the Company’s proposed Capital 
16 
Plan.  However, Central Hudson is proposing to modify the current SAIFI 
17 
negative revenue adjustment to a two-step structure.  As such, the 
18 
measurement of annual performance would include an NRA of 10 basis 
19 
points for exceeding the minimum threshold of 1.30 and an additional NRA 
20 
of 20 basis points for exceeding a maximum threshold of 1.34, which 
21 
maintains the existing maximum NRA of 30 basis points.  The proposed 
22 
two-step penalty level for SAIFI provides the Company with a financial 
23 
3304


--- Page 43 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE POLICY PANEL 
28 
 
incentive to improve reliability performance while maintaining aggressive 
1 
target levels that are aligned with historical results.   
2 
Central Hudson is also proposing changes to the Residential Customer 
3 
Satisfaction Survey and the PSC Complaint Rate in order to measure 
4 
customer sentiment more accurately and appropriately and better align the 
5 
Company’s metrics with statewide methodologies.  Finally, Central 
6 
Hudson is proposing modifications to metrics and new positive revenue 
7 
adjustments associated with Gas Safety. 
8 
IX. 
FORECASTING AND RATE DESIGN 
9 
Q.    
Does the Company propose changes to rate design in this filing? 
10 
A. 
Yes.  The Company is proposing to: 1) continue the elimination of gas tail 
11 
block rates; 2) eliminate the high volume usage discount offering for non-
12 
residential gas S.C. No. 6 customers; and 3) provide a Low Power 
13 
Attachment Program electric service offering to eligible customers within 
14 
S.C. No. 2.  
15 
Q.    
Please discuss the rationale for continuing the elimination of the gas 
16 
declining tail block rates and eliminating the S.C. No. 6 high volume usage 
17 
discount offering. 
18 
A. 
The elimination of the gas declining tail block rates and the elimination of 
19 
the S.C. No. 6 high volume usage discount improves the price signal of 
20 
delivery rate design and is aligned with the energy conservation and 
21 
emission reduction goals of the CLCPA.   
22 
3305


--- Page 44 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE POLICY PANEL 
29 
 
Q.    
Why is the Company proposing the Low Power Attachment Program 
1 
service at this time? 
2 
A. 
The Company proposes to add this service offering to provide a more 
3 
customer-friendly option and to meet increasing municipal customer 
4 
requests to attach small-powered devices to existing poles but who find 
5 
the cost of metering and new service installation required under the 
6 
Company’s current tariff to be difficult and cost prohibitive. 
7

MANAGEMENT AUDIT 
8

9

10

12

14

15

16 
that were modified by the Company.  As of the date of this filing, the 
17

18 
in accordance with the State Administrative Procedure Act (“SAPA”).

V. 
STAFFING AND EMPLOYEE DEVELOPMENT 
2 
Q.    
Please describe the Panel’s concerns regarding Staff’s adjustment to 
3 
Company staffing. 
4 
A. 
The Panel has concerns regarding Staff’s recommended adjustments to 
5 
headcount because they result in an insufficient employee headcount and 
6 
Staff’s rationale in support of the adjustments is flawed.  In particular, as 
7 
further described in the rebuttal testimonies of the Workforce 
8 
Compensation and Benefits Panel and Revenue Requirements Panel, 
9 
Staff’s adjustment to the Company’s labor distribution, which reflects a 
10 
three-year historical average and the application of an overly aggressive 
11 
and unreasonable vacancy rate, are based on faulty assumptions. 
12 
Q.    
Please describe the Panel’s concerns regarding Staff’s changes to the 
13 
Company’s base level headcount.   
14 
A. 
Staff proposed the removal of 119.5 full time equivalent (“FTE”) positions 
15 
or approximately 47% of the Company’s requested staffing increase.  
16 
Furthermore, 79 of these positions or approximately 66% of Staff’s 
17 
proposed reductions are within customer experience and are necessary to 
18 
implement monthly meter reading and collections activities while meeting 
19 
targeted customer service performance levels..  As discussed in the 
20 
rebuttal testimony of many of the Company’s Panels including the 
21 
Workforce Compensation and Benefits, Customer Experience, and 
22 
Technology Capital and Operations Panels, these staffing reductions 
23 
3326


--- Page 65 ---
Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419 
 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY OF THE POLICY PANEL 
8 
 
remove personnel necessary to support the Company’s operations.  
1 
Additionally, Staff’s proposed headcount reductions endanger the 
2 
Company’s ability to meet Customer Service Performance Indicators 
3 
(“CPSI”) targets and eliminate the employees necessary to conduct 
4 
monthly meter reading.  For these reasons, and those discussed 
5 
throughout the Company’s rebuttal testimony Staff’s proposed reductions 
6 
should not be accepted. 
7 
Q.    
Why does the Company disagree with Staff’s methodology for determining 
8 
the Company’s labor distribution? 
9 
A. 
As further described in detail in the rebuttal testimony of the Revenue 
10 
Requirements Panel, the use of a historical three-year average does not 
11 
reflect how the existing workforce allocated their time between expense 
12 
and capital, and it ignores the projected effect that incremental employees 
13 
will have on the overall labor distribution. 
14 
Q.    
Why does the Company disagree with Staff’s application of a vacancy 
15 
rate? 
16 
A. 
As further described in the rebuttal testimony of the Revenue 
17 
Requirements Panel, within these rate proceedings the determination of a 
18 
vacancy rate should be premised on a comparison of actual headcount to 
19 
that provided for in rates, since that represents the headcount for which 
20 
funds are being provided by customers.  As such, Staff’s calculation of a 
21 
vacancy rate that is based on the Company’s historic attrition rate is 
22 
fundamentally flawed.  In contrast when the historical vacancy rate is 
23 
3327


--- Page 66 ---
Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419 
 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY OF THE POLICY PANEL 
9 
 
calculated based on the headcount allowed in rates, it shows that the 
1 
Company was staffed at a level above the headcount allowed in rates, 
2 
which obviates the need for Staff’s recommended vacancy rate.  Finally, 
3 
within the forecasted period the Company staggered the hiring of 
4 
incremental employees across the Rate Year resulting in an impact very 
5 
similar to a vacancy rate imputation making Staff’s imputation redundant 
6 
and unnecessary.  For these reasons, Staff’s vacancy rate 
7 
recommendation should be rejected.

VI. 
SIGNIFICANT AND UNACCEPTABLE RISK ASSYMETRY 
10 
Q.    
Please describe the increased risk associated with the level of NRAs 
11 
proposed by SCSP. 
12 
A. 
The SCSP recommends a 414% increase in the one-year maximum 
13 
potential value for CSPI NRAs.    
14 
Q.    
Please describe the Panel’s concerns with the SCSP recommendation. 
15 
A. 
The SCSP’s proposed potential maximum CSPI NRA level is excessive 
16 
and overly punitive.  Additionally, as discussed within the rebuttal 
17 
testimony of the Company’s Customer Experience Panel, the CSPI 
18 
metrics are strongly correlated with one another, and it is inappropriate to 
19 
triple the NRA level for failure of more than one metric in the same year 
20 
given the relationship between the metrics.  The SCSP recommended 
21 
NRA levels in combination with existing potential electric and gas 
22 
operations NRAs produce a total maximum NRA level of 355 basis points 
23 
3328


--- Page 67 ---
Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419 
 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY OF THE POLICY PANEL 
10 
 
(“bps”) or 39% of Staff’s proposed 9.2% return on equity (“ROE”).  This 
1 
maximum NRA level represents significant financial uncertainty, which, as 
2 
discussed within the rebuttal testimonies of the Company’s Finance Panel 
3 
and Company Witness Nowak, is likely to lead to higher financing costs for 
4 
customers.   
5 
Q.    
Do you have any other comments on Staff’s proposed increase in NRAs? 
6 
A. 
Yes, as discussed previously, the SCSP testimony rejects many of the 
7 
Company’s proposed investments (e.g., technology and incremental 
8 
FTEs) that are required to maintain the current level of service that 
9 
customers expect.  The combination of Staff’s proposed increase in NRA 
10 
exposure and reduction in investments creates an untenable situation and 
11 
sets the Company up for failure.  If Central Hudson is going to be held 
12 
accountable for performance, it must be given the resources, tools and 
13 
ability to meet and exceed reasonable targets. 
14

15 
Company’s SAP transition? 
16 
A.

17 
Estimated Bill Credit.  Additionally, the Staff Policy Panel recommends the 
18 
following actions in consideration of “billing system issues”: 1) a zero 
19 
percent wage increase be imputed into the labor forecasts for executives; 
20 
2) for other employees, Staff recommends limiting rate recovery of wage 
21 
increases; and 3) Staff recommends denying the Company recovery of its 
22 
executive incentive compensation program.  These recommendations 
23

investment.

be penalized under such circumstances yet, the $2.4 million impact that is

14 bps reduction to the Company’s ROE.  Second, Staff Policy Panel’s

unjustly limit the Company’s ability to recover reasonably incurred

Q.    
Please explain how the Company developed the forecasted Rate Year 
1 
cash expenditures for the SIR sites. 
2 
A. 
The forecasted cash expenditures for the SIR sites were initially based on 
3 
a detailed cost modeling analysis last completed by an environmental 
4 
engineering consulting firm, ARCADIS, in December 2014.  The cost 
5 
modeling analysis involved assumptions relating to investigation 
6 
expenses, results of investigations, remediation costs, potential future 
7 
liabilities, and post-remedial operating, maintenance and monitoring costs, 
8 
and was based on a variety of factors including projections regarding the 
9 
amount and extent of contamination, the location, size and use of the 
10 
sites, proximity to sensitive resources, status of regulatory investigations, 
11 
and information regarding remediation activities at other MGP sites in New 
12 
York State.  The cost model also assumed that proposed or anticipated 
13 
remediation techniques are technically feasible and that proposed 
14 
remediation plans receive timely approval from the New York State 
15 
Department of Environmental Conservation (“NYSDEC”) and New York 
16 
State Department of Health (“NYSDOH”). 
17

Are the initial cash forecast expenditures further refined over time by the 
18 
Company?

A. 
As each site progresses from the investigation to the remediation phase, 
20 
the initial forecast expenditures are further refined.  Refinements are 
21

VI. 
SIR PROGRAM OVERVIEW 
15 
Q.    
Please provide an overview of Central Hudson’s SIR Program. 
16 
A. 
Central Hudson has an ongoing program for the management of its former 
17 
MGP sites and other non-MGP related environmental contamination sites.  
18 
The NYSDEC Remediation Division administers the laws and regulations 
19 
pertaining to the investigation and remediation process for all 
20 
contaminated sites under remedial programs.  In addition, they are 
21 
responsible for administrating remedial or corrective measures that may 
22 
3433


--- Page 172 ---
Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE SITE INVESTIGATION AND 
REMEDIATION PANEL 
11 
 
be necessary at sites that are permitted by the NYSDEC, under the 
1 
authority granted by the United States Environmental Protection Agency 
2 
(“USEPA”), to treat, store, and/or dispose of regulated hazardous wastes.  
3 
The NYSDEC regulates programs for Central Hudson sites, under a 
4 
NYSDEC Consent Order, the Brownfield Cleanup Program, or hazardous 
5 
waste storage permits.  The SIR process applicable to each respective 
6 
site, however, is carried out in accordance with a NYSDEC-issued 
7 
guidance document entitled “DER-10/Technical Guidance for Site 
8 
Investigation and Remediation” or provisions within the USEPA Resource 
9 
Conservation and Recovery Act (“RCRA”) Corrective Action Program for 
10 
permitted sites. Guidance provided in either case identifies the scope of 
11 
activities required to satisfy minimum requirements for the life cycle of the 
12 
site-specific remedial program, all of which seek to facilitate consistent, 
13 
accurate, efficient, and timely completion of remedial projects.  Central 
14 
Hudson’s role is to work in a collaborative process with all stakeholders for 
15 
the satisfactory remediation of each site incorporated into the clean-up 
16 
programs governed by the NYSDEC.  When Central Hudson, or its 
17 
predecessors, is considered the responsible party, Central Hudson will 
18 
take the lead throughout the process of investigating a site, developing the 
19 
appropriate remedial options, and, upon obtaining all the necessary 
20 
approvals, successfully remediating the site. 
21

Q.    
Please provide a brief background of Central Hudson’s SIR sites. 
1 
A. 
Central Hudson is currently wholly or partially responsible under New York 
2 
State and federal law for the following SIR sites: seven former MGP sites 
3 
(Newburgh, Laurel Street, North Water Street, Bayeaux Street, Kingston, 
4 
Catskill, and Beacon); one maintenance and warehouse facility (Elting’s 
5 
Corners); and one customer service office property (Newburgh Office).  
6 
With regard to the MGP sites, Central Hudson and its predecessors 
7 
owned and operated MGPs to serve their customers’ heating and lighting 
8 
needs.  These plants manufactured gas from coal and oil beginning in the 
9 
mid- to late-1800s. 
10 
Q.    
What is the status of those SIR sites? 
11 
A. 
Remediation has been completed at six of the seven former MGP sites, 
12 
and Elting’s Corners.  However significant remediation work related to 
13 
addressing impacted sediment in the Hudson River remains to be 
14 
performed at the former North Water Street Poughkeepsie MGP site.  With 
15 
regard to the Newburgh Office, although remediation of impacted soil has 
16 
been essentially completed, results from supplemental groundwater 
17 
investigations conducted during the current rate case agreement indicate 
18 
the need for further investigation.  Such investigation will likely include 
19 
installation of additional groundwater monitoring wells and, depending on 
20 
those results, some level of mitigation and/or remediation (e.g. passive 
21 
groundwater treatment) may be necessary. 
22 
3435


--- Page 174 ---
Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE SITE INVESTIGATION AND

Q.    
Is there anything of significance to note regarding North Water Street 
1 
MGP since testimony included in Case 20-E-0428 and Case 20-G-0429? 
2 
A. 
A hydraulic dredge pilot test was conducted in late 2020 and a summary 
3 
report was submitted to the NYSDEC in 2021.  The report concluded that 
4 
pursuing full-scale remediation using solely hydraulic dredging with all the 
5 
protective measures in place as tested, was not practical and alternative 
6 
options were to be evaluated.  While NYSDEC concurred and directed 
7 
Central Hudson to examine other methods, it still believed that removal of 
8 
source material is the best long-term remedy for the site.  That said, the 
9 
NYSDEC recognized that the remedy may have to be re-evaluated at its 
10 
basic level to determine if the extent of removal is feasible to achieve.  
11 
Subsequently, A Focused Remedial Alternatives Analysis (“FRAA”) was 
12 
submitted to the NYSDEC for review in late 2021.  
13 
Q.    
Was a response received from the NYSDEC regarding their review of the 
14 
FRAA? 
15 
A. 
Yes.  The NYSDEC and NYSDOH supplied several comments on the 
16 
FRAA in a letter dated April 8, 2022 that must be addressed before a new 
17 
RAA will be considered.  Additionally, they stated “given that the document 
18 
has been submitted prior to the completion of tests of alternate 
19 
containment methods for dredging, the document is rejected at this time.” 
20 
3436


--- Page 175 ---
Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE SITE INVESTIGATION AND 
REMEDIATION PANEL 
14 
 
Q.    
Please provide an update on any tests of alternate containment methods 
1 
for dredging conducted as requested by the NYSDEC. 
2 
A. 
Air Bubble Curtain (“ABC”) pilot test field and lab activities were conducted 
3 
throughout 2022 and the findings were presented to the NYSDEC during a 
4 
meeting in April 2023.  The presentation concluded that the ABC would be 
5 
ineffective at containing non-aqueous phase liquid (“NAPL”), turbidity, or 
6 
dissolved constituents either as a stand-alone or supplemental protective 
7 
measure.  As such no additional testing, specifically a full-scale pilot in the 
8 
Hudson River, is justified nor planned.  
9 
Q.    
Based on the updates provided above, what is the most likely path forward 
10 
for the project? 
11 
A. 
The NYSDEC stated they will reexamine the alternatives identified in the 
12 
FRAA before issuing any further comments or direction about the process 
13 
going forward.  Therefore, an agreed upon remedial approach is currently 
14 
undetermined and the timing of full-scale remediation has been deferred 
15 
beyond the Rate Year.   
16 
Q.    
Please address how the Company periodically assesses its SIR 
17 
procedures. 
18 
A. 
Central Hudson maintains its practice of obtaining qualified bids for the 
19 
type of service needed to ensure compliance with NYSDEC regulations 
20 
and PSC requirements.  Central Hudson continues to follow the same 
21 
protocols and procedures in place at the time of its last independent third-
22 
3437

Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419

REMEDIATION PANEL

party audit.  Further, the Company’s Financial Report, including SIR 
1 
amounts and disclosures, is subject to an annual audit by our external 
2

3

4 
including controls over purchasing and contracts, payment approvals, and 
5

6 
audits by both internal and external audit.

4

5 
Beacon’s Conservation Advisory Committee.  
6

7 
30-year-old multi-disciplinary design firm, and Atlas Studios is a creative community of 40+ 
8 
commercial tenants housed in a 100-year-old former textile factory in Newburgh, NY’s Historic 
9 
district. 
10 
 
11 
Q-2.  Are you a Central Hudson ratepayer?  
12 
A-2. 
Yes, including my home and business, I have five accounts with Central Hudson. 
13 
One for electricity use at my home in Beacon (I no longer have a fossil gas meter at my home), 
14 
and four for accounts at multiple properties in Newburgh, NY. Two of these Newburgh accounts 
15 
are electricity only, as I have discontinued fossil gas use in the building where those accounts are 
16 
located.

3442

6 
II. PURPOSE 
7 
Q-4.  What is the purpose of your testimony in this case? 
8 
A-4.  The purpose of my testimony is to use the narrative of my experience with BCAN 
9 
and as a commercial property owner and landlord to highlight the gap between the necessary 
10 
work required by the climate crisis and Central Hudson’s apparent unwillingness to engage in 
11 
that work on the one hand, and their efforts to actively undermine it on the other. 
12 
 
13 
Q-5.   In your opinion, what is the “necessary work required by the climate 
14 
crisis?”  
15 
A-5.  The necessary work required is the rapid phaseout of fossil fuels from the global 
16 
economy. 
17 
 
18 
Q-6.   In your opinion, what is the justification for calling this work “necessary”? 
19 
A-6.  The justification comes from the work of climate scientists across the world, 
20 
specifically those convened by the United Nations as members of the Intergovernmental Panel 
21 
on Climate Change (IPCC).  
22 
3443


--- Page 182 ---
4 
 
 
Recognizing the urgent need to keep global average temperatures as close as possible to 
1 
1.5 degrees C above preindustrial levels, and to that end, the urgent need to lower greenhouse 
2 
gas emissions that result from the combustion of fossil fuels, governing bodies at all scales of 
3 
leadership have set policy based on the recommendations of these scientists. 
4 
Furthermore, the reports of the IPCC have shown a crystal clear link between the 
5 
combustion of fossil fuels, the greenhouse gas emissions resulting from the combustion of fossil 
6 
fuels, to rising global average temperatures, and the connection of these rising temperatures to 
7 
extreme weather events. These weather events, and other climactic realities such as loss of access 
8 
to water and arable land due to drought conditions, result in direct, and dire consequences for 
9 
humans across the globe. As a result, leaders around the world have taken to referring to the 
10 
climate crisis as “an existential threat” and a “threat to humanity.” 
11 
It is therefore my opinion that it is appropriate to describe the work of phasing out fossil 
12 
fuels required to meet and prevent these existential threats, as necessary. 
13 
 
14 
Q-7.   In your opinion, what is the rationale behind denying the rate increase? 
15 
A-7.  Put simply, Central Hudson is not actively taking up the necessary work of the 
16 
climate crisis. It should not be rewarded for this inaction with a rate increase.  
17 
More specifically, as stated above, the climate crisis is real and is the result of the historic 
18 
and ongoing combustion of fossil fuels. Central Hudson is requesting a rate increase for fossil 
19 
gas delivery charges. Central Hudson is actively expanding the fossil gas network, which will 
20 
result in continued and increased fossil gas use. Central Hudson is promoting the adoption of 
21 
fossil gas equipment further expanding the fossil gas network, which will result in continued and 
22 
increased fossil gas use. Continued and increased fossil gas use is not in the best interest of 
23 
3444


--- Page 183 ---
5 
 
 
Central Hudson’s customers. The expansion of the fossil gas network is not in the best interest of 
1 
Central Hudson’s customers. The Public Service Commission is tasked with ensuring the best 
2 
interests of Central Hudson’s customers. 
3 
Therefore, it is in the best interest of Central Hudson customers for the Public Service 
4 
Commission (PSC) to deny the rate increase. 
5 
 
6 
III. CONTEXT 
7 
Q-8.   What is the global scale policy on fossil fuels? 
8 
A-8.  The global scale policy as stated in the most recent IPCC report1 calls for the 
9 
rapid phaseout of fossil fuels. 
10 
 
11 
Q-9.  What is the US policy on fossil fuels? 
12 
The US policy, as stated by the executive branch of the federal government2, is to rapidly 
13 
phase out fossil fuels.  
14 
 
15 
Q-10.  What is the policy of the state in which you reside on fossil fuels? 
16 
A-10.  The New York state policy, as stated in the Climate Leadership and Community 
17 
Protection Act (CLCPA) Scoping Plan3, calls for the rapid phaseout of fossil fuels. 
18 
 
19 
 
1 See: Climate Change 2023 Synthesis Report, Summary for Policymakers. Available at: 
https://www.ipcc.ch/report/ar6/syr/downloads/report/IPCC_AR6_SYR_SPM.pdf

room/statements-releases/2021/04/22/fact-sheet-president-biden-sets-2030-greenhouse-gas-pollution-reduction-
target-aimed-at-creating-good-paying-union-jobs-and-securing-u-s-leadership-on-clean-energy-technologies/ 
3 See: Scoping Plan of New York State Climate Action Council, Full Report, December, 2022. Available at:  
https://climate.ny.gov/-/media/Project/Climate/Files/NYS-Climate-Action-Council-Final-Scoping-Plan-2022.pdf  
3445


--- Page 184 ---
6 
 
 
Q-11.  What is the policy of the municipality in which you reside on fossil fuels? 
1 
A-11.  The policy of the city of Beacon, NY, as outlined in resolutions such as #22 and 
2 
#118 of 2020, and as illustrated by its behavior in following through on these resolutions, which 
3 
include the elimination of fossil fuels from city operations, and unanimously voting in legislation 
4 
such as local law #1 of 2023 (see Exhibit__(TW-01)) which prohibits the use of fossil fuels in 
5 
new construction and major renovations, calls for the rapid phaseout of fossil fuels. 
6 
 
7 
Q-12.  What is your personal policy regarding the use of fossil fuels? 
8 
A-12.  As a ratepayer, homeowner, and business owner, my personal policy regarding 
9 
the use of fossil fuels, as evidenced by my work in my community educating others on the 
10 
benefits of home electrification and the rapid phaseout of fossil fuels, campaigning for local 
11 
legislation that bans fossil fuels in new construction and major renovations in my hometown, 
12 
electrifying my home and workplace and vehicles that I drive and that our business uses as a way 
13 
of eliminating all fossil fuels from my home and workplace, calls for the rapid phaseout of fossil 
14 
fuels. 
15

Q-13.   What do you perceive Central Hudson’s attitude and approach are, when it 
17

18 
A-13.  The widespread practice of Central Hudson, shown through its behavior, its 
19 
marketing materials, its lobbying efforts, and its request for rate increases to be utilized for the 
20 
expansion of its fossil gas network, can only be characterized as a policy that actively encourages 
21 
the continued use of fossil gas.

IV. BCAN CAMPAIGN FOR FOSSIL FUEL-FREE BUILDINGS 
1 
Q-14.   How does the work of BCAN relate to the issues being considered in the rate 
2 
case? 
3 
A-14.  BCAN was formed in May of 2022 with the following vision: “In the face of the 
4 
climate crisis and the related crisis of care, our vision is to create systems of democracy, 
5 
resilience, and regeneration, resulting in a hopeful, joyful future of collective flourishing.” Its 
6 
very first campaign was a community-based effort to encourage city leaders to craft and pass 
7 
legislation that would prevent the use of fossil fuels in new construction and major renovations. 
8 
 
9 
Q-15.  What was your reason for engaging in this campaign? 
10 
A-15.  I engaged with this campaign as a way to achieve local action in the urgent work 
11 
required to mitigate the climate crisis. Specifically, I saw the campaign as a way to lower 
12 
greenhouse gas emissions by prohibiting the use of equipment that relies on the combustion of 
13 
fossil fuels.  
14 
It can be frustrating to match the urgency of the climate crisis with the scale of the 
15 
climate crisis. In spite of this, through my reading and research, I became aware that there were 
16 
ways to find action in our everyday lives that do in fact, make a difference in mitigation at the 
17 
global scale. 
18 
Electrification of buildings is a great example of action that individuals can take at the 
19 
scale of their home, or workplace that has impacts at larger scales. Replacing fossil-fuel 
20 
combusting machines with electric machines is by definition a rapid phaseout of fossil fuels.  
21 
3447


--- Page 186 ---
8 
 
 
Given the way that energy is supplied to our buildings, this phaseout has a broader, 
1 
systemic impact by reducing, albeit in a small way, the network of fossil fuel delivery. By 
2 
eliminating demand, the system of delivery is impacted. 
3 
At the same time, installing efficient, all-electric equipment is a way to encourage the 
4 
adoption of renewables, which deliver energy in the form of electricity. The world is seeing a 
5 
rapid growth in renewable energy on the supply side. This is due both to rapidly falling costs of 
6 
renewables, and policies put in place to accelerate the adoption of renewables. 
7 
In these ways, an individual act can lead to systemic impacts. 
8 
Targeting the emissions generated by buildings was a logical place to apply local efforts 
9 
since 30% of greenhouse gas emissions in NY State come from buildings.  
10 
In addition to targeting greenhouse gas emissions that result in the combustion of fossil 
11 
fuels, the campaign sought to reduce well-documented health risks stemming from the use of gas 
12 
for residential cooking. 
13 
 
14 
Q-16.  Given the attention to the climate crisis by state, national, and global leaders, 
15 
why did BCAN feel that it was necessary for their organization to campaign for fossil fuel-
16 
free buildings in Beacon? 
17 
A-16.  We saw a gap between the policies of leadership regarding the climate crisis, and 
18 
action regarding the mitigation of the climate crisis. In fact, there seemed to be an inverse 
19 
relationship between the scale of leadership, and the ability to achieve action on mitigation 
20 
policies at that scale.  
21 
We saw that local legislation was something that we could achieve action on at the pace 
22 
required by the climate crisis.  
23 
3448


--- Page 187 ---
9 
 
 
Q-17.  What was your role in the campaign? 
1 
A-17.  My role was to act as a liaison between BCAN, the Conservation Advisory 
2 
Committee (CAC), and members of the City Council. After an initial presentation of the 
3 
campaign by BCAN leadership to the CAC, I volunteered to apply my enthusiasm for seeking 
4 
local action to this role. 
5 
Tactically, we saw the CAC’s advisory role as a way to build “inside support” and 
6 
leverage that support to build a case for the council and mayor. As a member both of BCAN and 
7 
the CAC, I was well positioned to prepare and present explanatory materials on the gas-ban 
8 
legislation. 
9 
I prepared a document outlining the rationale for the support of the legislation by the 
10 
CAC and for the adoption of the legislation in Beacon. I presented the document to the CAC and 
11 
requested a vote on a letter of support. I also worked directly with members of the city council 
12 
on early drafts of the language of the legislation that was brought before the council, mayor, and 
13 
city administrator. 
14 
 
15 
Q-18.  Was the campaign successful? 
16 
A-18.  Yes. 6 months after the campaign began, the city council passed the legislation 
17 
with a unanimous vote on March 13, 2023. With the passage of the legislation Beacon became 
18 
the third city in New York state to adopt legislation prohibiting the use of fossil fuels in new 
19 
construction and major renovations, and the city with the earliest implementation timeline: 
20 
January 1 2024. 
21 
 
22 
Q-19. Why did the campaign include a restriction on all fossil-fuel types? 
23 
3449


--- Page 188 ---
10 
 
 
A-19.  According to the policies adopted by the UN, the US federal government, and the 
1 
New York state government, there is no distinction between fossil fuel types. Rather, there is a 
2 
universally accepted understanding that humanity needs to phase out all fossil fuels and replace 
3 
them with renewable energy sources.  
4 
In this way, the policies are not merely about choosing between bad options, but 
5 
eliminating all the bad options and choosing the good option.  
6 
 
7 
Q-20.  Would you say that the campaign was a mechanism for accelerating the 
8 
phaseout of all fossil fuels, and accelerating the adoption of renewable energy? 
9 
A-20.  I would say that yes, the campaign was a successful mechanism for accelerating 
10 
the phaseout of all fossil fuels, regardless of type.  
11 
It was also a mechanism for accelerating the adoption of renewable energy. Without the 
12 
option of fossil fuels in new construction and major renovations, these projects would be using 
13 
all-electric equipment. Given New York State’s commitment to aggressively clean up its 
14 
electrical grid, this all-electric equipment would be able to utilize increasing proportions of 
15 
renewable energy throughout its lifetime. An ability, it is worth noting again, that fossil fuel-
16 
combusting machines do not possess. 
17

V. EXPERIENCE AS BUSINESS OWNER, PROPERTY OWNER, HOMEOWNER AND 
19 
LANDLORD 
20 
 
21 
Q-21.  How is your experience as a homeowner, business owner, commercial 
22 
property owner, and landlord relevant to this testimony? 
23 
3450


--- Page 189 ---
11 
 
 
A-21.  It is relevant for several reasons. For starters, my experience shows that it is 
1 
possible to set personal energy policy that is aligned with global, national, and state policy to 
2 
phase out fossil fuels. Next, my experience can serve as an example of a homeowner, 
3 
commercial property owner, and landlord, who is actively eliminating fossil fuels from their 
4 
buildings and their business operations. It also provides an example of how individual choices 
5 
can be made that lead to wider systemic choices such as the reduction of the fossil fuel network. 
6 
Finally, my experience shows that there is demand among Central Hudson customers to rapidly 
7 
transition away from fossil fuels, such as I have. 
8 
This experience is relevant because, as stated in Section II above, this experience is the 
9 
story of an individual actively engaging in the necessary work of the climate crisis. 
10 
It is also relevant because, as I stated in Section IV above, this experience shows how 
11 
work at the scale of the individual can lead to results at larger scales. Using this same framing, 
12 
work at any scale can lead to results at larger scales. If the work is truly necessary, it is 
13 
incumbent upon anyone in a position to set or impact policy at their given scale, to take up this 
14 
work. 
15 
 
16 
Q-22.  What do you mean by “personal energy policy”?  
17 
A-22.  What I mean is that the choices about the type of energy my business uses are 
18 
mine alone to make. I don’t have to wait for a national or state policy to set policy regarding the 
19 
pace of fossil fuel phaseout in my life. Late in 2021, I decided that I wanted to eliminate fossil 
20 
fuels from my home and business, and I set a goal of one year to do that. 
21 
 
22 
3451


--- Page 190 ---
12 
 
 
Q-23.  How does this “personal energy policy” manifest itself in your home and 
1 
business? 
2 
A-23.  My home’s heat, air conditioning, and hot water are generated entirely by 
3 
efficient heat pump technology. My family and I cook our meals with an electric induction 
4 
range, and we dry our clothes using an electric dryer. We drive an electric vehicle and generate 
5 
electricity with our rooftop solar array. Whatever electricity we don’t generate with our home 
6 
array we source with 100% renewable energy. In February 2023, I requested that Central Hudson 
7 
remove the gas meter at my home. We have successfully eliminated fossil fuels from our home 
8 
energy needs.  
9 
Atlas Studios’ more than 40 tenants are housed in a 55,000 s.f., 100-year-old, 3-story 
10 
masonry building.  The building’s heat and air conditioning is entirely generated with efficient 
11 
electric heat pump technology, hot water is created with a combination of on-demand and hybrid 
12 
heat pump electric equipment.  Most of our electricity needs are generated by an off-site 
13 
Community Solar Array (CSA) with photovoltaic equipment that we own. What small amount of 
14 
electricity we don’t generate with this array, we source with 100% renewables. We have 
15 
requested that Central Hudson remove the building’s gas meter. There are no longer any fossil 
16 
fuels being utilized in the building, it is now 100% electrified.  
17 
For Atlas Industries, eliminating fossil fuels meant replacing a diesel-powered delivery 
18 
van with an all-electric model and replacing a gas range for an induction model in our office 
19 
kitchen. 
20 
  
21 
Q-24.  Why did you choose to stop utilizing fossil fuels in your home and business? 
22 
Was the decision based on the cost of fuel? 
23 
3452


--- Page 191 ---
13 
 
 
 
1 
A-24.  I feel confident that my testimony thus far has given ample rationale for 
2 
eliminating fossil fuels from my home and business. I would like to set aside the rationale of 
3 
climate crisis mitigation briefly and discuss operating costs.  
4 
The cost of fuel did play a part in this decision but not in the way that one might expect. 
5 
Unbelievably, there are those who would make a decision about what sort of heating equipment 
6 
to use based on short-term assessments of cost and benefit, and only on current costs of energy.  
7 
Given the forces aligned against continued fossil fuel use in our global, national, state, 
8 
and local economies, it is now quite obvious to anyone paying even loose attention that these 
9 
forces are going to raise the costs of fossil fuels. For decades, the market of fossil fuels is 
10 
volatile–up, down, and definitely not stable. At the same time, the cost of renewable energy has, 
11 
and will continue, to fall. Renewables are delivered in the form of electricity, therefore, 
12 
electricity costs will continue to fall. 
13 
Prices for fossil fuels will also rise because the costs of fossil fuels, which have been kept 
14 
historically low through subsidies and by ignoring externalities, are already seeing a reckoning 
15 
that requires the elimination of these subsidies, and the acknowledgment of loss and damage 
16 
created by the continued use of fossil fuels. Add to this the ongoing and increased number of 
17 
lawsuits that will be directed at fossil fuel extraction, refinement, and delivery entities, and there 
18 
is no reasonable scenario where the monetary costs of fossil fuels do not dramatically increase.  
19 
Lastly, as an increasing number of people, some encouraged and aided by rebates and tax 
20 
incentives like those being offered federally by the Inflation Reduction Act (IRA), and statewide 
21 
from New York State Energy Research & Development Authority (NYSERDA) adopt 
22 
equipment that can run on renewable energy, and as legislation such as Beacon’s gas-ban 
23 
3453


--- Page 192 ---
14 
 
 
legislation becomes more commonplace, there will be fewer users of fossil fuels. These users 
1 
will be disproportionately saddled with costs associated with a network designed and maintained 
2 
for many more users. 
3 
The timeline for these increases in costs for fossil fuel use is not on a distant horizon but 
4 
on a timeline equivalent to the life expectancy of equipment being installed today.  
5 
It is also worth pointing out that many, particularly those in disadvantaged communities, 
6 
suffer from a myopia of necessity. One living paycheck to paycheck may not have the liberty to 
7 
make decisions based on long-term costs. It is, therefore, incumbent upon institutions like the 
8 
PSC and utilities to help this population avoid these longer-term costs increases, and promote 
9 
options that are in their best interest in the longer term. Because of inevitable cost increases for 
10 
gas, and because of the inevitable burden of bearing disproportionate delivery costs as more and 
11 
more customers turn to electrification, accelerated electrification in disadvantaged communities 
12 
is a way to ensure that the phaseout of fossil fuels is equitable across socioeconomic strata. 
13 
Finally, there is the basic fact that fossil fuel-combusting equipment is less efficient than 
14 
their all-electric counterparts. Given that fossil fuel equipment consumes its energy source 
15 
entirely, it can at best reach efficiencies approaching 1, but not higher. Given the utilization of 
16 
the refrigerant cycle in heat pumps, energy is utilized, with a multiplier effect of achieving 
17 
efficiencies of at least 2-3 times that of fossil fuel-combusting machines even in cold climates4, 
18 
and in the case of ground source heat pumps, 5-6 times. 
19 
There is no reasonable rationale for installing gas equipment based on cost. Arguments in 
20 
favor of such behavior are not only myopic but ignorant of widely available facts. 
21 
 
4 See: Coming in from the Cold: Heat Pump Efficiency at Low Temperatures. Available at:  
https://www.cell.com/action/showPdf?pii=S2542-4351%2823%2900351-3 
3454


--- Page 193 ---
15 
 
 
When faced with a choice about which equipment to select, which equipment will cost 
1 
me more in the long run, or even merely over the lifetime of the equipment, I would certainly 
2 
choose equipment that can utilize the lower cost energy source of renewables, that is, electric 
3 
equipment. 
4 
Bringing back the rationale of climate crisis mitigation, the timeline relates to emissions 
5 
as well. The purchase and installation of fossil fuel-combusting equipment is a commitment to 
6 
emissions over the lifetime of that equipment. For anyone committed to the mitigation of 
7 
greenhouse gas emissions, this equipment is the most important equipment to eliminate, because 
8 
it is the equipment that will be in use, and emitting greenhouse gasses, the longest.  
9 
 
10 
Q-25.  In what ways did your work with the fossil fuel-free building legislation 
11 
campaign and your decision to electrify your home and workplace overlap? 
12 
A-25.  In both cases, the work was an example of creating a policy that results in the 
13 
phaseout of fossil fuels from buildings.  
14 
In the work I did electrifying home and workplace, the policy and implementation of 
15 
policy was applied to existing buildings. This is important work because of the sheer quantity of 
16 
existing buildings that need to be electrified. In NY state alone, approximately 6.2 million 
17 
buildings. 
18 
In the work I did with BCAN on the fossil fuel-free building legislation, the policy and 
19 
future implementation will be applied to new buildings. New buildings are a smaller part of the 
20 
building stock, but as noted above, it is the newest equipment that, if it combusts fossil fuels, will 
21 
be emitting greenhouse gas emissions the longest. As the old saying goes, the first rule of holes 
22 
is, if you find yourself in a hole, stop digging.  
23 
3455


--- Page 194 ---
16 
 
 
It is my sincere hope that both the legislation and the electrification efforts will serve as a 
1 
case study for similar efforts. Municipalities could use Beacon’s law as a template for their own 
2 
legislation. Building electrification efforts could serve as an example for commercial building 
3 
owners, landlords, and developers considering adaptive reuse projects involving older buildings 
4 
with historic design constraints. 
5

VI. SUPPORTING THE GOALS OF THE CLCPA 
7 
Q-26.  Given the CLCPA’s statement of commitment to a phaseout of fossil fuels, 
8 
what is your opinion regarding the role of a utility company in these efforts? 
9 
A-26.  A utility company can and should play a key role in the phaseout of fossil fuels.  
10 
It is my understanding that a public utility exists to serve customers safe, reliable, 
11 
affordable, and increasingly clean power by building and managing infrastructure, and 
12 
monetizing this infrastructure by charging ratepayers for its use.  
13 
The question for the PSC, the agency that regulates the activities of a for-profit, 
14 
monopoly is: is this activity serving to phase out the delivery of fossil fuels by ending the 
15 
construction of fossil-fuel delivery infrastructure, or is it serving to encourage the use of fossil 
16 
fuels by expanding fossil-fuel infrastructure? 
17 
If the answer to this question is the latter, then the utility company is not playing a 
18 
supporting role in the fossil fuel phaseout strategies outlined by the CLCPA. 
19 
 
20 
Q-27.  Given the CLCPA’s statement of commitment to a phaseout of fossil fuels, 
21 
what is your opinion regarding the role of the PSC in these efforts? 
22 
3456


--- Page 195 ---
17 
 
 
A-27.  Given that the CLCPA’s Scoping Plan is a statement of how climate action is in 
1 
the best interest of the people of the state of New York and, given that the CLCPA also clearly 
2 
illustrates through chapter 18 of the Scoping Plan titled “Gas System Transition” (see 
3 
Exhibit__(TW-02)) how climate action is tied to the phaseout of fossil fuels and, given that the 
4 
PSC is tasked with ensuring the best interests of the people of the state of NY, it follows that the 
5 
PSC must prioritize the goals of the CLCPA scoping plan. 
6 
 
7 
Q-28.  In your opinion, are the gas delivery rate increases in compliance with the 
8 
CLCPA?  
9 
A-28.  Given my testimony thus far, it should be very clear that my opinion is that the 
10 
gas delivery rate increases are not in compliance with the CLCPA.  
11 
Q-29.  Please elaborate on how the rate increases for gas delivery contradict the 
12 
goals of the CLCPA, and what you believe Central Hudson should do to align with it. 
13 
Any rate increase that was targeted toward the construction and expansion of the fossil 
14 
fuel distribution system is by definition not in compliance with the goals of the CLCPA.  
15 
Given that an investor-owned utility’s raison d’etre is the construction and expansion of 
16 
equipment to be used for the distribution of energy resources, any rate increases falling within 
17 
the portion of their business that concerns gas, will not be in compliance with the CLCPA. 
18 
Until Central Hudson has made a commitment towards the rapid phaseout of fossil fuels, 
19 
a commitment to decommissioning the existing fossil fuel network, and a good faith effort to 
20 
implement these commitments, Central Hudson is not in alignment with the CLCPA. 
21 
A clear statement of alignment has been provided by the PSC itself, in the form of Case 
22 
number 18-M-0084. Through this Order, dated July 20, 2023, the PSC has adopted a strategic 
23 
3457


--- Page 196 ---
18 
 
 
framework for the state’s building electrification portfolios. Quoting from a press release5 issued 
1 
the day of the Order’s effective date, the purpose of “(T)he revised and strategic framework and 
2 
associated policy direction (is to) guide program administrators’ proposals to ensure the future 
3 
(Building electrification) portfolios are in better alignment with the (CLCPA) and current 
4 
Commission clean energy policies. The order also directs the relevant electric and gas utilities 
5 
and the New York State Energy Research and Development Authority (NYSERDA) to file 
6 
proposals, responsive to the strategic framework, totaling $1 billion per year through the period 
7 
2026-2030, or $5 billion for the period.” In other words, the PSC, through this order, is insisting 
8 
that gas utilities such as Central Hudson, participate in building electrification initiatives that will 
9 
total, in aggregate, $1 billion per year as a way to bring their actions into alignment with the 
10 
CLCPA 
11 
 
12 
Q-30.  Besides the CLCPA, are there other statements of state policy regarding the 
13 
phaseout of fossil fuels? 
14 
Q-30.  Yes, the state constitution of New York.6 
15 
 
16 
Q-31.  Please explain. 
17 
A-31.  Given the well-documented detrimental impacts of fossil fuels on air and water 
18 
quality, and given the well-documented detrimental impacts to both the natural environment and 
19 
 
5  See: Press release regarding PSC order #18-M-0084 Available at:

public health of a rapidly warming planet, it is not a huge intellectual leap to conclude that the

1

A-34.  Yes, I have copies of several emails that I have received in the past 12 months.  
3 
Among other dubious claims, these emails declare that fossil gas is the better choice for 
4

9

10 
Resistance heat? Heat pump-generated heat? Is my indoor temperature of 70 degrees generated 
11 
by heat pumps less warm than the 70-degree indoor temperature generated by a gas furnace?   
12 
Ultimately, it is a brazen promotion of fossil gas over electricity. Why is a utility picking 
13 
“winners”? How can it pick a winner without specifying details about how the heat is generated?

15

16

29 
3460


--- Page 199 ---
21 
 
 
● FREE conversion estimate” 
1 
 
2 
Again this email makes no mention of air-source heat pumps or ground-source heat 
3 
pumps. These marketing materials are a comparison of fossil gas vs. heating oil vs. propane. As 
4 
noted above, these are all fossil fuels, and as such, are all energy types that must be phased out as 
5 
rapidly as possible. And yet, the intent of the email is to actively promote the adoption of fossil 
6 
gas. Why would a utility company be focusing on comparing energy types that are all slated for 
7 
phaseout? Why would the utility company purposefully omit a comparison to more efficient, 
8

9

I also received a letter from Central Hudson soon after the City of Beacon voted to adopt

12

13

14 
In addition to the points made above about the brazen promotion of fossil gas, this piece 
15 
of marketing is particularly pernicious because it is capitalizing on the local legislation banning 
16 
the use of fossil fuels in new construction and major renovations to sell more fossil gas 
17 
equipment and thereby expand fossil gas infrastructure.  
18 
At the risk of repetition, the sole purpose of the local legislation was to assist in the larger 
19 
efforts to phase out fossil fuels and stop the expansion of the fossil gas network. Central Hudson 
20 
instead used the occasion of the passage of this fossil fuel-ban legislation to promote fossil gas 
21 
over electrification. As far as I understand, Central Hudson has been asked not to promote fossil 
22 
gas over propane and oil. 
23 
3461


--- Page 200 ---
22 
 
 
Note that the chart included in the letter is a Heating-Source Cost Comparison. Again, 
1 
where is the comparison to cold-climate air-source heat pumps; to ground-source heat pumps? 
2 
As with the examples above, this chart includes a comparison of fossil fuel types. But the chart 
3 
does not say it is a comparison of fuel types. It compares “heating-source”. If that is the case, 
4 
why has Central Hudson left out other heating sources? The obvious answer is that it is actively 
5 
promoting fossil gas not just over other fossil fuels, but over more efficient heating sources. 
6 
 
7 
Note also the URL and email address given as a resource for benefits and conversion 
8 
eligibility: CentralHudson.com/SimplyBetter and SimplyBetter@CenHud.com. 
9 
Simply Better then what? Ground-source heat pumps? The recipient of this marketing is 
10 
left to believe that fossil gas is “Simply Better” than all other heat-sources, period. This is a key 
11 
component of the marketing. Central Hudson is telling its customers not to be concerned about 
12 
the current legislation in Beacon and its efforts to accelerate electrification and phase out fossil 
13 
fuels. Instead, they purport that their solution of gas is “SimplyBetter.” 
14 
 
15 
Q-35.  What was your reaction to these marketing materials? 
16 
A-35.  I found them to be incredibly frustrating, and in fact, personally insulting.  
17 
 
18 
Q-36. Explain. 
19 
A-36. It is both frustrating and insulting because it works in opposition to, and 
20 
undermines, what I know to be the necessary work of the climate crisis, namely, the rapid 
21 
phaseout of fossil fuels. necessary work towards which I have applied a great deal of personal 
22 
effort. 
23 
3462


--- Page 201 ---
23 
 
 
As I stated at the beginning of this testimony, the nature of the climate crisis, in regard to 
1 
causes, is well understood, and well documented. Furthermore, the solutions to the crisis are now 
2 
implemented in policies at all levels of leadership, from global to local.  
3 
The marketing of fossil fuels and promotion of continued use thereof, is therefore a 
4 
statement by Central Hudson of complete disregard for these policies, and indeed, for the work 
5 
that all rational people agree needs to be done. In particular, it is a statement of disregard for the 
6 
policies that have been implemented in the state in which they operate, New York, which has 
7 
laid out very clearly, in the CLCPA and its scoping plan, a roadmap for the phaseout of fossil 
8 
fuels. 
9 
 
10 
VIII. CONCLUSION 
11 
Q-37.  Are there specific alternatives or solutions you would like to propose to 
12 
address the issues you've raised regarding Central Hudson's environmental alignment and 
13 
promotional practices? 
14 
A-37.  My recommendations are below as follows: 
15 
1. Central Hudson should not be allowed to expand the fossil gas network. 
16 
2. Central Hudson should not be allowed to promote fossil gas. 
17 
3. Central Hudson’s environmental alignment should be directly reviewed by the 
18 
Climate Action Council. Alignment, or lack thereof, should guide any decisions 
19 
by the PSC regarding requested rate increases. 
20 
 
21 
Q-38.  In your opinion, is the proposed rate increase for gas delivery appropriate at 
22 
this time? If not, please explain. 
23 
3463


--- Page 202 ---
24 
 
 
A-38.  As I hope that I have made clear in my testimony, the proposed rate increase for 
1 
gas delivery is absolutely not appropriate at this time. 
2 
An additional point that I would like to add to my testimony goes beyond the direct 
3 
correlation between the rate increase and the expansion of the fossil gas network. Any rate 
4 
increase is not just money in the abstract, but money coming from ratepayers.  
5 
Therefore, Central Hudson is not merely asking for a rate increase on gas delivery, it is 
6 
asking for permission to use ratepayer money to expand the fossil gas network. By using this 
7 
increase in marketing materials that promote the use of fossil gas, and equipment that burns 
8 
fossil gas, it is asking its ratepayers to fund the marketing of fossil gas. 
9 
In other words, Central Hudson is asking ratepayers to fund activities that are not, as I 
10 
hope my testimony has illustrated, in their best interest. These are ratepayers, like myself who 
11 
have actively decided to engage in the necessary work of the climate crisis, who have set a 
12 
personal energy policy that eliminates fossil fuels from their homes and businesses, and who 
13 
actively worked in their community to accelerate the phase out of fossil fuels and the adoption of 
14 
renewables, and have sought out ways in which to multiply those efforts by working to educate 
15 
others on this experience. These are ratepayers who seek out opportunities to support and expand 
16 
on the policies established by the CLCPA in New York State, federal climate policies of the 
17 
United States government, and global policies as set forth by the United Nations. 
18 
The decision lies with the Public Service Commission, the body in New York State 
19 
tasked with regulating the for-profit monopoly that is Central Hudson on behalf of the public it is 
20 
sworn to serve.  
21 
3464


--- Page 203 ---
25 
 
 
It is my hope that anyone reading this testimony will rightly conclude that the only 
1 
firm “no.”

appropriate response to Central Hudson’s request for a rate increase on fossil gas delivery is a

Review Process 
13 
Q. 
Please describe the general nature of your 
14 
review process. 
15 
A. 
Our review process focused on the need, timing, 
16 
and effectiveness of the Company’s physical and 
17 
cyber security projects and programs.  We 
18 
reviewed the justification provided by the 
19 
Company in its testimony and exhibits, and 
20 
analyzed the Company’s responses to IRs in order 
21 
to assess whether a proposed project is 
22 
necessary for the provision of safe and reliable 
23 
service.  In addition to assessing the need for 
24 
3506


--- Page 245 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Security Panel 
 
 
6 
 
each project and program reviewed, we considered 
1 
whether the timing of the project’s inclusion in 
2 
the Company’s security posture was consistent 
3 
with industry best practices.  We compared the 
4 
capabilities of the Company’s programs with 
5 
industry standards and best practices, and 
6 
looked at possible alternatives to assess their 
7 
flexibility and scalability to match a threat 
8 
landscape that can change significantly over a 
9 
short period of time.

Q. 
What standards and best practices do you 
7 
reference for your review of cybersecurity? 
8 
A. 
The Panel relies on the National Institute of 
9 
Standards and Technology’s, or NIST, 
10 
Cybersecurity Framework, or CSF, as the basis of 
11 
our review.  It is a recognized collection of 
12 
flexible and comprehensive standards, 
13 
guidelines, and practices related to cyber 
14 
security, which includes Special Publications, 
15 
or SP, that provide additional, specific 
16 
cybersecurity-related guidelines, technical 
17 
specifications, recommendations, and reference 
18 
materials.  We also reference the Center for 
19 
Internet Security’s “Critical Security 
20 
Controls;” the NIST-funded MITRE Corporation’s 
21 
“Adversary, Tactics, Techniques and Common 
22 
Knowledge (ATT&CK)” framework; the TSA Pipeline 
23 
Security Guidelines; and NERC CIP standards.  
24

Physical Security Proposals

18 
Q. 
What characteristics and standards are 
19 
considered when determining whether a utility 
20 
site or asset is “critical”? 
21 
A. 
The NERC CIP-014-3 requires operators “To 
22 
identify and protect Transmission stations and 
23 
Transmission substations, and their associated 
24 
3509


--- Page 248 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Security Panel 
 
 
9 
 
primary control centers, that if rendered 
1 
inoperable or damaged as a result of a physical 
2 
attack could result in instability, uncontrolled 
3 
separation, or Cascading within an 
4 
Interconnection.”  Sites and facilities 
5 
identified under this standard are considered 
6 
“critical” and must be adequately protected from 
7 
threats to physical security.  Additionally, a 
8 
site or asset may be considered “critical” by 
9 
OREP USS when it provides essential service to 
10 
unrelated critical infrastructure, such as 
11 
hospitals. 
12
