Cyber Security Proposals 
1 
Q. 
Please summarize the Company’s proposals with 
2 
respect to cyber security. 
3 
A. 
The Company proposals consist of several line 
4 
items.  For ease of reference, we have grouped 
5 
them into three general categories.  The first 
6 
category is Network Segregation projects and the 
7 
second category is Hardware and Software 
8 
projects.  These projects are generally 
9 
described in the Company’s response to DPS-258, 
10 
Attachment 1.  The third category includes 
11 
several incremental cybersecurity Full Time 
12 
Equivalents, referred to as FTEs. 
13 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s cybersecurity 
14 
Network Segregation capital proposal. 
15 
A. 
The Company proposes to continue and expand the 
16 
work it started in its previous rate case to 
17 
segregate its networks and to upgrade the 
18 
infrastructure that supports them.  This work 
19 
includes upgrading the components of the 
20 
Company’s network architecture, providing 
21 
redundant communications paths to endpoints in 
22 
its wide area network, or WAN, and replacing 
23 
equipment that is approaching end of support, 
24 
3517


--- Page 256 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Security Panel 
 
 
17 
 
such as routers.  In addition to the router 
1 
replacement, the Company also proposes to 
2 
acquire software tools that assist in managing 
3 
and monitoring its networks. 
4 
Q. 
Does the Panel agree with the Company’s 
5 
proposal? 
6 
A. 
Yes. 
7 
Q. 
Why does the Panel agree with the Company’s 
8 
proposed network segregation activities? 
9 
A. 
Network segregation, in and of itself, is a 
10 
cybersecurity best practice and an important 
11 
part of building a multi-layered cybersecurity 
12 
defense.  Network segregation can be viewed as 
13 
the equivalent of putting up walls to close off 
14 
parts of the network from each other, only 
15 
allowing access to those parts by individuals 
16 
with proper permission.  The Company is also 
17 
increasing its network resilience by ensuring 
18 
endpoints on its WAN have redundant connections.  
19 
In the event that one connection is interrupted 
20 
or blocked, the redundant pathway will allow the 
21 
endpoint to continue to share the network 
22 
instead of suffering a loss of communications. 
23 
Q. 
Why does the Panel agree with the Company’s 
24 
3518


--- Page 257 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Security Panel 
 
 
18 
 
proposal to replace routers and acquire new 
1 
software tools? 
2 
A. 
As shown in its response to DPS-638, the Company 
3 
is replacing a model of router in its networks, 
4 
because those routers will reach end-of-support 
5 
in 2027.  Devices that have reached end-of-
6 
support no longer have security patches 
7 
developed and released by the device 
8 
manufacturer.  Patching vulnerabilities in 
9 
devices’ operating systems and firmware is a 
10 
cornerstone of cybersecurity.  It is therefore a 
11 
best practice to remove devices that are out of 
12 
support from the network, and replace them with 
13 
supported devices.  The Panel also agrees with 
14 
the Company’s proposal to acquire software tools 
15 
that assist in managing and monitoring its 
16 
networks.  Network segmentation greatly 
17 
increases the complexity of managing and 
18 
monitoring a network, so tools that assist and 
19 
automate as much of the work as possible reduce 
20 
the need for vendors, contractors, or FTEs to 
21 
operate the more complicated network. 
22 
Q. 
Does the Panel have any adjustments to this 
23 
proposal? 
24 
3519


--- Page 258 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Security Panel 
 
 
19 
 
A. 
No.  Based on our review, we believe the 
1 
proposal is a necessary investment for the 
2 
Company to make to maintain its current level of 
3 
security. 
4 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s cybersecurity 
5 
Hardware and Software capital proposals. 
6 
A. 
In the TCOP’s testimony, on pages 36 to 37 and 
7 
beginning on page 45, and in the Company’s 
8 
response to DPS-258, the Company proposes several 
9 
cybersecurity hardware and software line items.  
10 
These include Identity and Access Management, or 
11 
IDAM, tools and upgrades, ServiceNow security 
12 
tools, and a collection of other cybersecurity 
13 
tools.  The Company provided additional details 
14 
regarding these proposals in its responses to 
15 
DPS-556, DPS-567, DPS-568, DPS-748, and DPS-749. 
16 
Q. 
What is IDAM? 
17 
A. 
According to NIST SP 800-63-3 through 63-3B, 
18 
Identity and Access Management is a fundamental 
19 
and critical cybersecurity capability that seeks 
20 
to ensure the correct people and things have the 
21 
correct access to the correct resources at the 
22 
correct time.  Successful implementation of 
23 
Identity and Access Management limits the 
24 
3520


--- Page 259 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Security Panel 
 
 
20 
 
possibility of unauthorized external access to a 
1 
utility’s networks.  In the event unauthorized 
2 
access does occur, it would mitigate the impact 
3 
by limiting access to narrow sections of the 
4 
network and data.  It would also cause an 
5 
intruder to make more “noise” in the network if 
6 
they attempt break into other sections, so they 
7 
would be more likely to be detected, and 
8 
detected earlier. 
9 
Q. 
What is the Company’s justification for its 
10 
proposed IDAM implementation? 
11 
A.

Q.

4

5

6 
tools that the Company proposes will offer 
7 
improvements over what it currently employs, and 
8 
the Panel agrees with the Company’s stated 
9 
reasons for its proposed IDAM upgrades. 
10 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s proposed 
11 
ServiceNow investment. 
12 
A. 
According to pages 36 to 37 of the TCOP’s 
13 
testimony, Central Hudson has formally adopted 
14 
the NIST CSF as its Cybersecurity governance 
15 
framework.  This framework requires Central 
16 
Hudson to centralize Governance, Risk, and 
17 
Compliance, or GRC, tracking, which is currently 
18 
being performed manually across many different 
19 
solutions, business processes, and teams.  The 
20 
Company states that, in support of this 
21 
initiative, the ServiceNow’s GRC module would 
22 
provide policy and control tracking, risk 
23 
management, and audit management.  The Company’s 
24 
3522


--- Page 261 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Security Panel 
 
 
22 
 
response to DPS-749 further discusses the 
1 
proposed role of ServiceNow in mitigating the 
2 
impact of attacks through improved preventative, 
3 
detective, and deterrent controls. 
4 
Q. 
Does the Panel agree with the Company’s proposed 
5 
use of ServiceNow tools? 
6 
A. 
Yes.  BEGIN CONFIDENTIAL INFORMATION <
 
7 
 
8 
 
9 
 
10 
 
11

Health Care Costs 
1 
Q. 
Please describe how the Company forecasted its 
2 
Rate Year health insurance costs. 
3 
A. 
As discussed on page 15 of the Company’s Revenue 
4

5 
healthcare and pharmacy medical insurance costs 
6 
(excluding dental and vision) are based on a 
7

8 
witness.”  
9 
Q. 
Do you agree that health care costs should be 
10 
escalated using a separate inflation index from 
11 
that which is used to escalate cost items in the 
12 
general inflation pool? 
13 
A. 
No. This is counter to the Commission’s long-
14 
standing practice, which is to escalate health 
15 
care costs by the GDP-PI.  This policy was first 
16 
established in Commission Opinion No. 84-27, 
17 
issued October 12, 1986. 
18 
Q.  Please explain this long-standing practice. 
19 
A. 
Utilities’ health care costs, along with many 
20 
other cost categories, are typically pooled and 
21 
escalated based on a “consensus forecast” of the 
22 
GDP-PI.  This index is a broad measure of 
23 
inflation faced by producers in the economy, 
24 
3538


--- Page 277 ---
Case 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
 
5 
 
meaning the index covers a large array of costs.  
1 
Q. 
Does the GDP-PI reflect increases in health care 
2 
costs? 
3 
A. 
Yes.  Health care costs constitute a significant 
4 
portion of the costs included in the GDP-PI. 
5 
Thus, changes in the GDP-PI reflect the large 
6 
percentage increases in health care costs faced 
7 
by companies such as Central Hudson.  
8 
Q. 
Do all of the cost components of the GDP-PI 
9 
escalate at the same rate? 
10 
A. 
No.  Exhibit__(DSG-2) shows that some components 
11 
of costs in the GDP-PI that have increased at a 
12 
rate lower than the overall average GDP-PI rate. 
13 
However, the Company is expected to manage the 
14 
cost increases in the entire inflation pool 
15 
covered by the GDP-PI, rather than predicting 
16 
changes in health care costs separately from the 
17 
other cost components in its general inflation 
18 
pool. 
19 
Q. 
Are there recent Commission orders where the 
20 
Commission reaffirmed its long-standing practice 
21 
to escalate health care costs by the GDP-PI? 
22 
A. 
Yes, in the Order Establishing Rates, issued 
23 
June 26, 2014, in Case 13-W-0295, United Water 
24 
3539


--- Page 278 ---
Case 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
 
6 
 
New York, Inc., or UWNY, the Commission 
1 
reiterated and elaborated on its position 
2 
regarding this issue.  
3 
Q. 
How did the Commission characterize its position 
4 
on this issue in that order? 
5 
A. 
Specifically, the Commission stated, “[w]hat 
6 
UWNY and the R[ecommended] D[ecision] have 
7 
failed to recognize is that in the generic 
8 
inflation pool, by its nature, some costs are 
9 
fully expected to increase faster than general 
10 
inflation rates while others are not. If medical 
11 
expense falls into the former category, as 
12 
experience indicates according to UWNY, that 
13 
does not alter the fact that the generic 
14 
inflation rate is an average of inflationary and 
15 
deflationary changes in the prices of all the 
16 
various goods and services in the pool. Thus, a 
17 
deviation between the increase in a single 
18 
pooled cost item such as medical expense, 
19 
relative to the average increase, does not 
20 
justify removing that item from the pool in an 
21 
attempt to predict it separately. On the 
22 
contrary, pooling of diverse expenses into a 
23 
single inflation forecast tends to minimize the 
24 
3540


--- Page 279 ---
Case 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
 
7 
 
forecasting errors--and as noted in [a separate 
1 
Commission] decision, the waste of time and 
2 
resources--that would result from attempting to 
3 
predict each expense item separately.” In this 
4 
decision, the Commission also stated that “we 
5 
would expect the utility companies to have 
6

7 
their resources more productively to other 
8 
matters.”

Transmission Right-of-Way Vegetation Management 
5 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s transmission ROW 
6 
vegetation management program.   
7 
A. 
The Company’s transmission ROW vegetation 
8 
management program covers seven ROW maintenance 
9 
activities: ROW floor maintenance, mowing and 
10 
access roads, side trimming, danger tree 
11

12

13 
floor maintenance component is a cycle-based 
14 
integrated vegetation management, or IVM, 
15 
program and the sideline component is a priority 
16 
and scheduled cycle based on numerous variables.   
17

What level of funding does the Company propose 
18 
for transmission ROW vegetation management for 
19 
the Rate Year?

21

22

23

24

following years to account for inflation and 
1

3

4

5

10

14 
Company performs a ground patrol annually on 
15 
each bulk transmission line and any line 
16 
segments identified for mid-cycle review.

Q. 
Please describe the Company’s ROW edge 
3

4 
A.  ROW edge reclamation consists of surveying and 
5 
clearing areas associated with the acquisition 
6 
of additional easements where the company had 
7 
less than optimal ROW widths.  These locations 
8 
are identified by reviewing discrepancies 
9 
between the Plan and Profile drawings, which are 
10 
construction plans showcasing both a top-down 
11

12 
facility, and the Company’s actual easement 
13 
rights.  This work was originally identified in 
14 
2009 from a review of the Company's Plan and 
15 
Profile drawings for each transmission line.  
16 
The edge reclamation work is typically completed 
17 
in conjunction with routine ROW maintenance or 
18 
as additional ROW widths are acquired.  Edge 
19 
reclamation work is similar to side trimming and 
20 
is affected by many of the same factors.  Some 
21 
of this work is also associated with removing 
22 
large trees in residential yards that previously 
23 
were not considered part of the ROW and were not 
24 
3575


--- Page 314 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
SEVMP

1

2 
working around structures associated with yards 
3 
such as sheds, swimming pools, landscaped areas, 
4 
garages, and fenced in areas.  These factors 
5 
result in an increased cost per mile associated 
6 
with edge reclamation.  The Company's total Rate 
7 
Year expenditure forecast for ROW edge 
8 
reclamation, which includes completing its 
9 
previously addressed carry over miles, is 
10 
$825,229.

14

15 
territory.

Company’s Forecasting Methodology 
16 
Q. 
How did the Company develop its electric and gas 
17 
customers and sales forecasts? 
18 
A. 
The Company generally used an econometric 
19 
modeling-based approach to develop its electric 
20 
and gas forecasts.  Using historical monthly 
21 
data, the Company estimated customer count 
22 
models, and energy use per customer models.  
23 
These models estimate the relationship between 
24 
3589


--- Page 328 ---
Case 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Forecasting Panel 
 
 
11 
 
customer growth and energy usage to explanatory 
1 
drivers such as weather (i.e., degree days), 
2 
economic conditions, price, and other 
3 
explanatory factors.  The Company then used 
4 
those econometrically estimated models to 
5 
forecast customer growth and energy use per 
6 
customer.  Finally, the Company multiplied its 
7 
monthly forecasts of customers by its forecasts 
8 
of monthly energy use per customer to arrive at 
9 
monthly forecasts of energy sales.  These models 
10 
and forecasts were separately developed at the 
11 
service class level of disaggregation. 
12 
Q. 
Please describe the basic functioning of the 
13 
econometric forecasting models used in this 
14 
proceeding. 
15 
A. 
These econometric models relate a dependent or 
16 
response variable, in this case, sales per 
17 
customer or the number of customers, to a set of 
18 
explanatory or independent variables.  These 
19 
explanatory variables include weather, economic, 
20 
demographic, and other seasonal and cyclic 
21 
variables.  Weather variables are represented by 
22 
heating degree days, or HDD, or cooling degree 
23 
days, or CDD.  Seasonal variables may adjust for 
24 
3590


--- Page 329 ---
Case 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Forecasting Panel 
 
 
12 
 
calendar month or other billing-cycle-related 
1 
variations.  The economic and demographic 
2 
explanatory variables are the structural 
3 
components of the econometric models. 
4 
Q. 
Please explain the non-structural, time-series 
5 
component of forecasting models. 
6 
A. 
The term “non-structural component” refers to 
7 
elements or features of the model that lack 
8 
strict economic interpretations.  These 
9 
components are primarily utilized for pattern 
10 
extraction from the data, rather than direct 
11 
economic analysis.  This is performed by 
12 
including moving average, or MA, and 
13 
autoregressive, or AR, variables to the model 
14 
specifications.   
15 
Q. 
Please describe the Statistically Adjusted End-
16 
Use, or SAE, approach used by the Company’s 
17 
Forecasting and Rates Panel. 
18 
A. 
On page 18 of their direct testimony, the 
19 
Company’s Forecasting and Rates Panel describes 
20 
its use of SAE variables.  Briefly, the panel 
21 
states that its forecasts developed for the 
22 
electric residential and commercial classes and 
23 
all firm gas classes utilized SAE models.  The 
24 
3591


--- Page 330 ---
Case 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Forecasting Panel 
 
 
13 
 
panel states that the SAE approach integrates 
1 
structural changes in end-use saturation and 
2 
efficiency trends and addresses the interaction 
3 
of economic variables through the construction 
4 
of end-use variables such as heating, cooling 
5 
and others.  
6 
Q. 
How are these “SAE” variables and other 
7 
variables developed? 
8 
A. 
The Company provided the technical definition of 
9 
these and other constructed variables in its IR 
10 
responses to DPS-548, DPS-550, and DPS-551.  The 
11 
Company developed its SAE variables using 
12 
proprietary software algorithms.  These software 
13 
algorithms combined Company-specific data along 
14 
with demographic and economic data from other 
15 
sources to produce the SAE variables. 
16 
Q. 
How did the Company use these SAE variables as 
17 
part of its forecasting methodology? 
18 
A. 
The Company used these SAE variables as 
19 
independent variables in its models, taking the 
20 
place of economic variables like price or 
21 
territory gross domestic product, or GDP.  
22 
Q. 
Are you objecting to the use of end-use data? 
23 
A. 
No.  We are concerned about the mixture of 
24 
3592


--- Page 331 ---
Case 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Forecasting Panel 
 
 
14 
 
collected Company-specific data and third-party 
1 
provided data wrapped into one variable. 
2 
Q. 
What is your concern with this approach? 
3 
A. 
Our concern is that this end-user metering and 
4 
appliance saturation information is being 
5 
combined with other variables to produce 
6 
composite variables. Since the SAE variables are 
7 
composites, it is not possible to evaluate the 
8 
reasonableness of the effect of each input 
9 
variable.  
10 
Q. 
Please provide an example. 
11 
A. 
The Company’s four residential and commercial 
12 
electric deliveries models have three types of 
13 
SAE variables called Xheat, Xcool, and Xother.  
14 
These SAE variables were constructed using 
15 
weather, price, and economic data, in addition 
16 
to end-use variable data from the Company’s 
17 
meters and from the Company’s own residential 
18 
appliance saturation and/or energy management 
19 
surveys.  The end-use data are used to relate 
20 
the Company’s own appliance saturation and 
21 
efficiency trends to estimates from mixed data 
22 
from the U.S. Energy Information Administration 
23 
estimates for the Middle Atlantic Census Region.  
24 
3593


--- Page 332 ---
Case 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Forecasting Panel 
 
 
15 
 
The economic data used in creating these “X” 
1 
variables, obtained from Moody’s Analytics, were 
2 
blended into the X-variables along with weather 
3 
data, and assumptions about customers’ fixed 
4 
price elasticity and income elasticity.  
5 
Q. 
Please elaborate on your concern with the 
6 
Company’s use of these SAE variables. 
7 
A. 
Our concern over the use of these variables is 
8 
twofold:  First, the use of these variables 
9 
results in a non-transparent, uninterpretable 
10 
model.  Second, the use of these variables posit 
11 
the existence of fixed relationships between 
12 
variables that are not estimated within the 
13 
model. 
14 
Q. 
Please expand on the first concern. 
15 
A. 
Since these SAE variables were developed from 
16 
numerous data sources, it is not clear what is 
17 
the impact of each of the underlying factors on 
18 
the total demand for energy.  For example, for 
19 
its residential gas non-heat use per customer 
20 
model, the Company relies on an SAE variable 
21 
named “ResNHtVars.HuseMul.”  Oddly, this is 
22 
labelled as a “Heating end use variable” in the 
23 
Company’s response to DPS-550, Attachment 2.  
24 
3594


--- Page 333 ---
Case 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Forecasting Panel 
 
 
16 
 
This variable is a combination of a residential 
1 
price index, a residential price elasticity 
2 
value, an income elasticity measure, a number of 
3 
households variable, a household size variable, 
4 
and a billed degree day variable.   
5 
Q. 
What is the problem with aggregating so many 
6 
variables into a single SAE variable? 
7 
A. 
Aggregating such a large number of variables 
8 
into a single explanatory variable could 
9 
potentially complicate statistic and overall 
10 
model interpretations.  It also implies that 
11 
there are fixed relationships between 
12 
explanatory variables when there potentially are 
13 
none.  This could potentially lead to inaccurate 
14 
forecasts that fit historical data well, but for 
15 
improper reasons, for example due to spurious 
16 
correlations embedded within the SAE variables. 
17 
Q. 
Did the Company provide similar additional 
18 
information on how it created its other SAE 
19 
variables? 
20 
A. 
Yes, in the Company’s response to DPS-550, the 
21 
Company provided the formulae used for the 
22 
creation of all these variables.   
23 
Q. 
Were these data helpful for the Panel to develop 
24 
3595


--- Page 334 ---
Case 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Forecasting Panel 
 
 
17 
 
an understanding of how or why the Company used 
1 
SAE variables in its forecasting methodology? 
2 
A. 
Partially.  While the Panel appreciates the 
3 
Company’s efforts at transparency in providing 
4 
the equations used to calculate its SAE 
5 
variables, the Panel could not find a 
6

7

8 
marginal change in the variables included in the 
9 
SAE variables. 
10 
Q. 
Did the Company otherwise explain its 
11 
justification for using the SAE variable method? 
12 
A. 
On page 19 of the Company’s Forecasting and 
13 
Rates Panel testimony, the Company asserts that 
14 
traditional regression models based on macro-
15 
economic variables cannot explain the current 
16 
trend declining electric sales despite positive 
17 
economic growth over the last decade. 
18 
Q. 
Does the Panel agree with this criticism of the 
19 
traditional regression models? 
20 
A. 
No, we do not.  While it is true that the 
21 
relationship between dependent and independent 
22 
variables may change over time, there are ways 
23 
to capture this change in relationship.  
24 
3596


--- Page 335 ---
Case 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Forecasting Panel 
 
 
18 
 
Additionally, the use of SAE variables does not 
1 
alleviate this concern, it just includes a 
2 
priori assumptions, or assumptions presumed to 
3 
be true based on previous knowledge or belief 
4 
and not empirically derived with the current set 
5 
of data, on the relationships between variables 
6 
in a non-transparent manner.   
7 
Q. 
Can you give an example of how a model can be 
8 
formulated to capture changing relationships 
9 
between variables? 
10 
A. 
Yes.  In the Panel’s electric residential non-
11 
heat model, discussed later in our testimony, a 
12 
quadratic form is used to capture the marginal 
13 
changes in the relationship between households 
14 
and residential non-heat customers. 
15 
Q. 
Please expand upon your second concern with the 
16 
Company’s use of SAE variables. 
17 
A. 
The SAE variables represent implicit a priori 
18 
assumptions on the relationships between the 
19 
variables used in the sales forecast.  In other 
20 
words, the use of SAE variables implies the 
21 
existence of fixed relationships between 
22 
explanatory variables that are not modeled in 
23 
the Company’s reported models.   
24 
3597


--- Page 336 ---
Case 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Forecasting Panel 
 
 
19 
 
Q. 
What is the issue with this approach? 
1 
A. 
The use of these SAE variables complicates model 
2 
interpretation, making it difficult to make a 
3 
determination of the reasonableness of the 
4 
results. 
5 
Q. 
What is the Panel’s recommendation regarding 
6 
these SAE variables? 
7 
A. 
We recommend that underlying variables be 
8 
included as stand-alone explanatory variables on 
9 
the right-hand side of the forecasting equation.  
10 
In this manner, the importance of each 
11 
underlying variable to the level of energy used 
12 
by customers can be discretely estimated, 
13 
consequently allowing both a statistical 
14 
analysis.  
15

16 
models?

A.

18 
electric customers and sales and gas customers 
19 
and sales.

20

more accurately.  For example, in our

3600

6

Residential Heat Models 
7 
Q. 
Please describe both the Company’s and the 
8 
Panel’s residential heat customer forecasts. 
9 
A. 
The Company’s model covers data from January 
10 
2016 through December 2022 and forecasts an 
11 
average of 28,675 customers and 18,890 MWh for 
12 
the rate period.   
13 
Q. 
Please describe the Panel’s residential heat 
14 
customer forecasts. 
15 
A. 
Our model forecasts an average of 28,915 
16 
customers and 19,811 MWh for the rate period. 
17 
Q. 
What are the primary drivers of the Company’s 
18 
forecast? 
19 
A. 
The Company’s residential customer heat forecast 
20 
is driven primarily by a household trend 
21 
variable. 
22 
Q. 
What is a household trend variable? 
23 
A. 
A household trend variable does not use the 
24 
3607


--- Page 346 ---
Case 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Forecasting Panel 
 
 
29 
 
number of households in an area, but instead 
1 
chooses an index year to compare households to, 
2 
based on trends in changes in households over 
3 
time. 
4 
Q. 
Is this a valid way to incorporate changes in 
5 
households over time in an econometric model? 
6 
A. 
Yes, although its interpretation is slightly 
7 
different than using households directly.  When 
8 
incorporating the number of households in the 
9 
model, the marginal effect of a household can be 
10 
calculated by taking the derivative of the fit 
11 
model with respect to households.  Conversely, 
12 
an indexed variable reveals the relationship 
13 
between the percent change in the quantity of 
14 
households over time and calculating the 
15 
marginal effect of a household would involve 
16 
taking the derivative of the estimated equation 
17 
with respect to the index multiplied by the 
18 
derivative of the index with respect to 
19 
households.   
20 
Q. 
Does the Panel’s model incorporate a household 
21 
trends variable? 
22 
A. 
No.  Our model is an autoregressive time trend 
23 
model that includes two time-trended variables 
24 
3608


--- Page 347 ---
Case 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Forecasting Panel 
 
 
30 
 
to capture both the trend and changes in the 
1 
trend of customers.  
2 
Q.  Please explain the Company’s residential heat 
3 
UPC forecast model. 
4 
A. 
The Company’s residential heat UPC forecast 
5 
model is primarily driven by SAE variables.  
6 
These variables, described previously, are 
7 
constructed from a variety of economic and other 
8 
variables calculated by the proprietary 
9 
algorithms of the software used by the Company. 
10 
Q. 
How does the Panel’s residential heat UPC 
11 
forecast model differ from the Company’s model? 
12 
A. 
Our model for residential heat UPC forecast does 
13 
not use the SAE variables.  Instead, our model 
14 
is driven by electricity price and heating 
15 
degree days. 
16 
Q. 
Please explain the concept of model good-of-fit 
17 
statistics. 
18 
A. 
Goodness-of-fit statistics are a measure of how 
19 
well a particular model matches the historical 
20 
data it is modeled upon.   
21 
Q. 
Are goodness-of-fit statistics a good measure of 
22 
how the model will perform outside of the model 
23 
data? 
24 
3609


--- Page 348 ---
Case 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Forecasting Panel 
 
 
31 
 
A. 
Goodness-of-fit statistics are backwards looking 
1 
and while they are a useful tool in model 
2 
selection, these statistics should not be used 
3 
as an indicator of future performance of the 
4 
model. 
5 
Q. 
What is an r-squared statistic? 
6 
A. 
The r-squared statistic, ranging from zero to 
7 
one, measures the degree of goodness-to-fit of 
8 
the regression model, with zero being the least 
9 
fit and one being the best fit.   
10 
Q. 
What is the range of adjusted r-squared measures 
11 
of the Company’s models? 
12 
A. 
The adjusted r-squared measures of the Company’s 
13 
models range from 0.87 to 0.99, suggesting that 
14 
approximately 87 to 99 percent of variations in 
15 
deliveries and customer counts are explained by 
16 
the included explanatory variables.   
17 
Q. 
How does the Panel’s model r-square statistic 
18 
compare to the Company’s for this customer 
19 
group? 
20 
A. 
The Company’s model achieves a very high r-
21 
square of 0.972.  This is in contrast with our 
22 
model, which achieves an r-square of 0.869. 
23 
Q. 
What do these scores mean in terms of the 
24 
3610


--- Page 349 ---
Case 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Forecasting Panel 
 
 
32 
 
accuracy of the Panel’s or the Company’s model? 
1 
A. 
They are an indicator of how well the specified 
2 
model fits the historical data.  It is crucial 
3 
to note that a high backwards-looking fit does 
4 
not guarantee future performance.  Therefore, 
5 
the use of easily interpretable variables are 
6 
important, because an important part of 
7 
forecasting is evaluating the reasonableness of 
8 
the results. 
9 
Residential Non-Heat Models 
10 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s residential non-
11 
heat customer forecasts. 
12 
A. 
According to the Company’s model, the average 
13 
number of residential non-heat customers is 
14 
forecasted to be 237,407 in the Rate Year.  This 
15 
model is primarily driven by a trended household 
16 
variable.   
17 
Q.  Please describe the Panel’s residential non-heat 
18 
customer forecasts. 
19 
A. 
Our model predicts an average of 232,568 
20 
customers and is driven by households and 
21 
households squared.  It also includes two 
22 
autoregressive terms. 
23 
Q. 
As noted earlier in your testimony, please 
24 
3611


--- Page 350 ---
Case 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Forecasting Panel 
 
 
33 
 
expand on the quadratic included in this model 
1 
to capture the marginal changes in the marginal 
2 
effect of households on customer counts.   
3 
A. 
The Panel used a quadratic to model both the 
4 
marginal effect and the change in the marginal 
5 
effect by including both log(households) and 
6 
log(households)^2.  This addition loosens the 
7 
assumption of a constant linear relationship 
8 
between households and customers, which may not 
9 
be applicable for a number of reasons including 
10 
changes in housing stock and electrification. 
11 
Q. 
How do you interpret the positive sign on 
12 
log(households) and the negative sign on 
13 
log(households)^2? 
14 
A. 
In the case of this model, the coefficients 
15 
imply that the marginal effect of additional 
16 
households on residential non-heat customers is 
17 
decreasing. 
18 
Q. 
How does this quadratic feature work to produce 
19 
that effect? 
20 
A. 
Since the coefficient on log(households) is much 
21 
larger than the coefficient on 
22 
log(households)^2, the initial marginal effect 
23 
of changes in households has a positive effect 
24 
3612


--- Page 351 ---
Case 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Forecasting Panel 
 
 
34 
 
on customers. As the number of households 
1 
increases, log(households)^2 grows faster than 
2 
log(households), causing the marginal effect of 
3 
households to decrease as it increases. 
4 
Q. 
Why might this be the case? 
5 
A. 
There are a number of possible reasons, 
6 
including changes in housing stock, or an 
7 
increased number of residential heat customers 
8 
as electrification continues. 
9 
Q. 
What are the Company’s and this Panel’s sales 
10 
forecast for residential non-heat sales? 
11 
A. 
The Company predicts residential non-heat sales 
12 
of 150,248 MWh.  Staff’s forecast calls for 
13 
151,330 MWh in the rate year. 
14 
Q. 
Please describe any differences between 
15 
Company’s and the Panel’s residential non-heat 
16 
UPC forecast model. 
17 
A. 
The Company’s model is driven by SAE variables.  
18 
Our model is driven by electricity price and 
19 
household income.  
20 
Q. 
What are some advantages of the Panel’s model 
21 
over the Company’s? 
22 
A. 
In the Panel’s opinion, its model is more 
23 
straightforward to interpret due to its direct 
24 
3613


--- Page 352 ---
Case 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
Staff Forecasting Panel 
 
 
35 
 
use of economic variables instead of the 
1 
combined SAE variables, which have a more 
2 
complex interpretation that can make it 
3 
difficult to isolate the effect of specific 
4 
factors, as described previously.

12 
Q. 
What are post-model adjustments? 
13 
A. 
Post-model adjustments are intended to adjust a 
14 
forecasting model for incremental changes in 
15 
trends that are not reflected in the historic 
16 
data upon which the econometric forecasting 
17 
model was estimated, such as changes in policy 
18 
that are expected to increase or decrease the 
19 
rate of adoption going forward relative to the 
20 
existing trend of adoption.  For example, the 
21 
existing rate of adoption reflected in the 
22 
recent trends of EVs do not take into account 
23 
policies and programs geared toward greatly 
24 
3639


--- Page 378 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff UFPP 
 
 
5 
 
increasing EV adoption that were not in place 
1 
historically. 
2 
Q. 
What effect do post-model adjustments have on 
3 
the sales forecast? 
4 
A. 
The direct effect of each type of post-model 
5 
adjustment varies by the nature of the 
6 
adjustment.  For example, since EVs and HPs tend 
7 
to cause electric sales to increase, omitting 
8 
the EV and HP post-model adjustments would cause 
9 
the forecast of electric sales to be too low. 
10 
Conversely, since increased PV adoption tends to 
11 
cause electric sales to decrease due to self-
12 
supply and energy export, omitting the PV post-
13 
model adjustment would cause the forecast of 
14 
electric sales to be too high. 
15 
Q. 
Has the Panel now identified any concerns with 
16 
how the Company calculated its post-model 
17 
adjustments? 
18 
A. 
Yes, we identified two needed corrections.  
19 
First, the Company did not properly account for 
20 
existing trends in PVs and EVs when developing 
21 
its electric post-model adjustments.  
22 
Specifically, the Company inappropriately 
23 
adjusted its model outputs by cumulative levels 
24 
3640


--- Page 379 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff UFPP 
 
 
6 
 
of adoption of PVs and EVs.  For PVs, the 
1 
Company based its adjustment on the cumulative 
2 
installed PV since December 2001 for residential 
3 
non-heat customers, July 2004 for commercial 
4 
non-demand customers, and December 2002 for 
5 
commercial demand customers.  For EVs, the 
6 
Company’s adjustment reflected the cumulative 
7 
level of EVs registered since 2022.  Second, the 
8 
Company’s post-model adjustment calculations for 
9 
PVs, EVs, and HPs do not reflect the inclusion 
10 
of the most recent nine months of available 
11 
actual data through September of 2023, which 
12 
Staff included in its model estimation. 
13 
Q. 
Did Staff alert the Company of these errors? 
14 
A. 
Yes, in Staff’s response to IR CH-010. 
15 
Q. 
Why is it inappropriate for the Company’s post-
16 
model PV and EV adjustments to reflect the 
17 
adoption of PVs and EVs that accumulated over 
18 
the historic model estimation period? 
19 
A. 
Because the existing trends in EV and PV 
20 
adoption are already contained within the 
21 
historical data, it is only necessary to adjust 
22 
the forecast results by the amount of future 
23 
sales that would be incremental to the existing 
24 
3641


--- Page 380 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff UFPP 
 
 
7 
 
trend that is captured by the econometric 
1 
modeling process.  Otherwise, there would be a 
2 
double counting of the historical impact. 
3 
Q. 
How did the Panel correct this error? 
4 
A. 
While the Panel still relied upon the Company’s 
5 
base post-model adjustment workpapers, we 
6 
modified these adjustment calculations to 
7 
reflect only the adoption of EVs and PVs 
8 
incremental to the trend instead of their 
9 
cumulative adoption levels.  That is, we 
10 
modified the Company’s post-model adjustment 
11 
calculations to reflect only growth in PVs and 
12 
EVs that is not reflected in the historical data 
13 
upon which the PV and EV models were estimated.  
14 
The impacts of these modifications are reflected 
15 
in Exhibit__(SUFPP-4). 
16 
Q. 
Please explain your second correction. 
17 
A. 
Since the SFP estimated its models on data which 
18 
included the most recent nine months of 
19 
available actual data through September 2023, we 
20 
similarly modified the Company’s PV, EV, and HP 
21 
post-model adjustments, which were based on 
22 
actual data through December 2022, to reflect 
23 
this additional nine months of more recent 
24 
3642


--- Page 381 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff UFPP 
 
 
8 
 
actual data.

10 
Design Day Demand and Supply 
11 
Q. 
Please describe how the Company procures gas 
12 
supply for its service territory. 
13 
A. 
Central Hudson utilizes a combination of 
14 
multiple pipeline suppliers, which deliver 
15 
natural gas to its four city gates where the gas 
16 
is brought into its service territory, to serve 
17 
its firm customer demands throughout the year.  
18 
The Company also uses a physical storage option 
19 
for its gas supply which it fills or withdraws 
20 
from depending on the time of year and system 
21 
needs.  During the summer months, contracted gas 
22 
volumes that are higher than what is needed to 
23 
fill the base load requirements on the system 
24 
3652


--- Page 391 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Riebel 
 
 
5 
 
are placed into storage where it can be 
1 
withdrawn during the winter months when the 
2 
demand increases.  This physical hedge is 
3 
designed to dampen price volatility and is a 
4 
common practice throughout New York gas 
5 
utilities. 
6 
Q. 
How does the Company determine how much gas 
7 
supply it requires for its service territory? 
8 
A. 
The Company forecasts firm sales gas demand on 
9 
its system every day throughout the year and 
10 
contracts for gas supply to meet that forecasted 
11 
demand.  While the non-heat portion of that 
12 
demand is fairly consistent throughout the year, 
13 
the winter months include a particularly high 
14 
demand heating component which can vary greatly 
15 
over that period depending on the temperature. 
16 
Q. 
Please explain the concept of a design day. 
17 
A. 
A design day is essentially the day on which a 
18 
gas utility system historically experiences its 
19 
coldest average temperature.  Central Hudson 
20 
uses negative eight degrees Fahrenheit as its 
21 
design peak day.  While this occurs very 
22 
infrequently, it is this design day for which 
23 
Central Hudson plans. 
24 
3653


--- Page 392 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Riebel 
 
 
6 
 
Q. 
Based on your review, is the Company prepared to 
1 
meet upcoming heating season demand and design 
2 
day requirements? 
3 
A. 
Yes, it is. 
4 
Q. 
Does the Company utilize an outside party to 
5 
assist it in managing its supply portfolio? 
6 
A. 
Yes.  Central Hudson releases available capacity 
7 
to an Asset Manager, who looks to offer excess 
8 
capacity to the marketplace where it can extract 
9 
value for that excess and share it with Central 
10 
Hudson’s ratepayers.  This practice is typical 
11 
in New York State and is a tool that helps gas 
12 
utilities provide customers with a benefit for 
13 
assets that are not needed throughout the year. 
14

Alternatives to Traditional Infrastructure Investment 
23 
Q. 
Describe the types of programs and projects the 
24 
3663


--- Page 402 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Riebel 
 
 
16 
 
Company is proposing that transitions the 
1 
Company away from traditional infrastructure 
2 
investments.  
3 
A. 
The Company is proposing programs and projects 
4 
related to NPAs, RSG, and hydrogen.  The Company 
5 
also generally discusses RNG. 
6 
Q. 
What are NPAs? 
7 
A. 
Non-pipe alternatives are potential solutions to 
8 
providing customers with the energy needed for 
9 
their home or business while avoiding 
10 
traditional gas infrastructure investment.   
11 
Q. 
Describe the Company’s proposal related to NPAs. 
12 
A. 
Central Hudson has been engaged in NPAs for 
13 
numerous years, pursuant to the rate plans 
14 
adopted in its previous two gas rate cases (20-
15 
G-0429 and 17-G-0460).  These projects have 
16 
concentrated on LPP elimination, where customers 
17 
along such segments of pipe are converted from 
18 
natural gas to another form of energy, like 
19 
electricity.  The Company proposes to continue 
20 
these efforts for the Rate Year ending June 30, 
21 
2025.   
22 
Q. 
What is your recommendation? 
23 
A. 
I agree that the Company should continue to 
24 
3664


--- Page 403 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Riebel 
 
 
17 
 
pursue opportunities for NPAs throughout its gas 
1 
system while following the guidelines that were 
2 
established by the Commission in its rate orders 
3 
issued in the previous Central Hudson rate 
4 
cases. 
5 
Q. 
What is RSG? 
6 
A. 
Responsibly Sourced Gas, also referred to as 
7 
Certified Natural Gas, is natural gas that has 
8 
been documented as having been extracted and 
9 
handled in a manner that is more environmentally 
10 
friendly than the traditional gas process.  It 
11 
is a way for a gas utility to help reduce the 
12 
climate impact of its energy choices by reducing 
13 
measurable emissions while providing purchasers 
14 
with proof that they are making impactful 
15 
changes to its emission reduction goals.    
16 
Q. 
Describe the Company’s historic and future plans 
17 
for RSG.  
18 
A. 
The Company’s Climate Leadership and 
19 
Sustainability Panel, and the Procurement Panel, 
20 
both discuss the Company’s plans for 
21 
incorporating RSG into its supply mix.  The 
22 
Company previously purchased RSG in the summer 
23 
of 2022 as a pilot program.  The Company 
24 
3665


--- Page 404 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Riebel 
 
 
18 
 
indicates that it has also procured RSG as part 
1 
of its base supply in 2023.  In response to DPS-
2 
523, the Company further states that it flowed 
3 
the cost of the 2023 RSG through its Gas Supply 
4 
Charge since the cost was at or below the 
5 
weighted average base and storage portfolio cost 
6 
of gas.  In this rate case, the Company is 
7 
requesting permission to continue to pursue RSG 
8 
even if costs exceed the weighted average cost 
9 
of gas, up to a limit of $200,000.      
10 
Q. 
What are your recommendations on the request to 
11 
continue purchasing RSG in the Rate Year?  
12 
A. 
I agree with the Company’s proposal.  The 
13 
previous purchases of RSG were made with limited 
14 
impact to customers, since those purchases were 
15 
made at a level that equated to the Company’s 
16 
overall weighted average cost of gas.  Although 
17 
the Company is asking for permission to purchase 
18 
RSG at a unit level which could exceed the 
19 
weighted average cost of gas, the Company has 
20 
stated that these incremental costs would not 
21 
exceed $200,000.  Based on the fact that the 
22 
exposure to customers would be no more than 
23 
$200,000, and that RSG can be a means to 
24 
3666


--- Page 405 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Riebel 
 
 
19 
 
decrease greenhouse gas emissions, I agree with 
1 
the Company’s proposal.  That notwithstanding, I 
2 
also recommend that the Company be required to 
3 
file the details of the purchases of RSG going 
4 
forward.  Those details should include the name 
5 
of the certifier of the RSG, the source of gas 
6 
that is being certified, the volume, and the 
7 
cost per unit.  This information should be filed 
8 
with the Secretary on a monthly basis during the 
9 
Rate Year, to ensure that Staff can properly 
10 
track and monitor these RSG purchases. 
11 
Q. 
What is RNG? 
12 
A. 
Renewable Natural Gas is a term used to describe 
13 
biogas that comes from a variety of sources, 
14 
including solid waste landfills, digesters at 
15 
wastewater treatment plants, livestock farms, 
16 
food production facilities and organic waste 
17 
management operations.  It is produced through a 
18 
biochemical process, such as anaerobic 
19 
digestion, or through thermochemical means, such 
20 
as gasification.   
21 
Q. 
Did the Company include in its rate filing a 
22 
proposal regarding RNG? 
23 
A. 
While the Company’s Procurement Panel indicated 
24 
3667


--- Page 406 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Riebel 
 
 
20 
 
that the Company has considered RNG when looking 
1 
for alternatives to traditional gas 
2 
infrastructure investments, it appears that the 
3 
Company has yet to include Renewable Natural Gas 
4 
as part of its supply portfolio and is not 
5 
requesting to do so for the Rate Year. 
6 
Q. 
Do you have any recommendations on RNG for the 
7 
Company? 
8 
A. 
Although the Company has been considering RNG, 
9 
it has yet to provide a concrete plan to add 
10 
this alternative supply as part of its future 
11 
plans.  As part of its plans going forward, I 
12 
recommend that the Commission require Central 
13 
Hudson take a comprehensive look at its service 
14 
territory for opportunities where RNG could be 
15 
produced, and identify the benefits and costs 
16 
associated with those opportunities.  The 
17 
Company should be directed to provide its 
18 
findings in a report to be filed with the 
19 
Secretary to the Commission.    
20 
Q. 
Summarize the Company’s plans for hydrogen. 
21 
A. 
Central Hudson proposes a Clean Hydrogen 
22 
Feasibility Study, with the objective of 
23 
identifying portions of its distribution system 
24 
3668


--- Page 407 ---

Riebel

where hydrogen blending could be successful and 
1

2 
gas heating and process load.  The Company plans 
3

4

5

6

22

24 
3669

Riebel

DIRECT TESTIMONY OF JESSICA MULLEN 
1 
I. Introduction and Qualifications 
2 
Q-1.  State your name, title, and affiliation. 
3 
A-1. 
Jessica Mullen, Executive Director, Communities for Local Power. Additionally, 
4 
I’m a landlord in Ulster County, and have 3 Central Hudson accounts. 
5 
 
6 
Q-2.  Provide a summary of your professional background and experience. 
7 
A-2.  I graduated from SUNY New Paltz over 20 years ago, with a Bachelors of Arts in 
8 
International Relations and Political Science. Around that time, I started working for the League 
9 
of Conservation Voters (LCV), as a field organizer and team leader. At the National level, LCV 
10 
monitors federal elected officials on how they vote when it comes to environmental issues 
11 
(including whether or not they miss a vote). During this time, I was in Florida, doing voter 
12 
outreach for issues related to the environment and climate change. This included surveying folks 
13 
on what different social, economic, and political issues mattered to them, and through the 
14 
decades, much has changed. Climate issues are more noticeably affecting people, and since the 
15 
early days of my work, both the demand and necessity for climate solutions and a just transition 
16 
off fossil fuels are much more prevalent now. 
17 
I’ve worked on campaigns and with coalitions that have each focused on various issues 
18 
relating to the climate crisis– mitigating the effects of it, and reducing its causes. I advocate for 
19 
policy that accelerates inclusive clean energy solutions and moves New York away from 
20 
dependency on fossil fuels. I’ve worked for New Yorkers Against Fracking, against fracked oil 
21 
pipelines proposed to go through 5 counties in New York, down into New Jersey, against “bomb 
22 
trains” carrying explosive crude oil, and have opposed various proposed gas plants. In 
23 
3683


--- Page 422 ---
CASE NO. 23-E-0418/23-G-041 
 
3 
2019/2020, I facilitated the administration of an air quality monitoring project sponsored by the 
1 
Southwest Environmental Health Project in Dutchess County, New York. We installed various 
2 
air monitors near the Cricket Valley gas plant, to track VOCs (volatile organic compounds) and 
3 
PM2.5 (particulate matter). Using the findings, I created reports and infographics based on the 
4 
findings, for elected officials, residents, and educators. This material has been a helpful resource 
5 
for other communities facing the negative health impacts of gas infrastructure (See Exhibit__JM-
6 
04).  
7 
I’ve also spent an equal amount of time advocating for solutions to help get us off of 
8 
fossil fuel. I initiated and facilitated the Village and Town of New Paltz’s applications for New 
9 
York's Climate Smart Communities Certification, and then trained for the Clean Energy 
10 
Communities follow-up program.  I’ve created syllabi based on New York State energy policy 
11 
for SUNY New Paltz Interns who received 3 credits and stipend, organized state-wide student 
12 
conference for renewable energy, assisted with the launching and operation of the Solarize 
13 
Hudson Valley Campaign, and have organized various green expos, energy efficiency events, 
14 
and electric vehicle fairs.  
15 
From its inception, I was a member of NY Renews, a coalition that was crucial in 
16 
advocating for and passing what became New York’s groundbreaking climate policy, the 
17 
Climate Leadership and Community Protection Act. I’m also a Cornell Cooperative Extension 
18 
Volunteer Energy Navigator, having completed NYSERDA’s (NY State Energy and Research 
19 
Development Authority) 6-month program.1 Training topics included: Energy and 
20 
Environmental Justice, Climate Science, Understanding Your Own and Others Energy Use, 
21 
 
1 “Energy Navigator Volunteers,” Cornell Cooperative Extension https://ccedutchess.org/energy/energy-navigator-
volunteers  
3684

4 
Building Science and Energy Audits, Energy Efficiency, Renewable Energy, Heating Systems, 
1 
Outreach and Community Building, and Transportation.  
2 
Most recently, in addition to energy affordability and utility accountability, I work on 
3 
green workforce development, advocate for policies that prioritize energy efficiency and healthy 
4 
air quality in homes, and I will be serving on Ulster County’s Renewable Energy Implementation 
5 
Planning Committee, which is being formed by the county so they can meet the CLCPA mandate 
6 
of 70% renewable energy production by 2030 (See Exhibit__JM-03).

II. Purpose of Testimony and Recommendation 
9 
Q-3.  What is the purpose of your testimony?  
10 
A-3.  The purpose of my testimony is to oppose the Central Hudson rate increase. I also 
11 
want to illustrate that Central Hudson has not met the terms of the last rate case, and that they are 
12 
unfit to serve as a utility provider if New York State is serious about lowering its gas emissions. 
13 
 
14

III. Billing Issues 
14 
Q-5.  Have you observed any billing problems issues among community members 
15 
in relation to their energy bills with Central Hudson? 
16 
A-5.  Yes– multiple billing for the same period, overbilling, absence of billing– it’s 
17 
been a huge problem for both businesses and residents. Unfortunately, Central Hudson’s claims 
18 
that the billing issues have been resolved are not the reality. We’re still contacted by folks who 
19 
are having the same problems reported a few years ago.  
20 
 
21 
Q-6.  What do you tell community members who contact you and inform you that 
22 
they're still experiencing billing issues? 
23 
3686


--- Page 425 ---
CASE NO. 23-E-0418/23-G-041 
 
6 
A-6.  We advise them to contact their state representatives, to report it to the Public 
1 
Service Commission (PSC), and to call the Public Utility Law Project (PULP) hotline since 
2 
PULP has a closer handle on what they should do. If it’s an overbilling issue, we also usually 
3 
suggest that in the meanwhile, they pay some of what they owe, like $100 for instance, but do 
4 
not pay all of it. We specifically tell them not to pay off a balance that’s incorrect, or seems 
5 
incorrect. We make it a point to tell community members this.  
6 
 
7 
Q-7.  Why do you make a point of telling community members not to pay off an 
8 
incorrect balance? 
9 
A-7.  Because unless the Public Service Commission, the Governor, or other decision 
10 
makers do something about it, these poor people will never see that money again. How many 
11 
households and businesses have paid an incorrect bill, because they felt they had to? How many? 
12 
We don’t know. This has been going on for years– it feels like there’s no end in sight. And those 
13 
that have overpaid…what’s happened? Nothing.  
14 
 
15 
Q-8.  What do you think should happen with the rate case, and with the overbilling 
16 
issues? 
17 
A-8.  Well first- this rate case shouldn’t even be permitted to happen right now. Central 
18 
Hudson is still under investigation for their business practices. That needs to be dealt with before 
19 
anything else can happen. 
20 
But since the rate case is underway, the proposed increases not only need to be denied, 
21 
but there must be a decrease. A decrease isn’t unheard of in a rate case either– it happened with 
22 
the last Central Hudson rate case.  In 2020, Central Hudson asked for increases of $7.76 per 
23 
3687


--- Page 426 ---
CASE NO. 23-E-0418/23-G-041 
 
7 
month for electricity. In the Order Adopting the Terms of the Joint Proposal for that rate case,2 
1 
the PSC instead approved a three-year plan in which residential customers paid 33 cents less per 
2 
month for electricity during the first year and monthly increases of $1.72 and $1.82 for the final 
3 
two years. The proposed increase now, in comparison with the last request, is 287% greater for 
4 
the delivery of electricity. Central Hudson’s track record in providing the public with electricity, 
5 
and their inability to fulfill the terms of past agreements, need to be taken into consideration 
6 
when determining if any rate increase, or, for that matter, a decrease is warranted. 
7 
Other things should happen too – financial reparations must be made to ratepayers. 
8 
There’s widespread overbilling still occurring; it needs to be stopped, and reversed. All 
9 
ratepayers should receive a credit to start, and then, an independent computer and accounting 
10 
forensics firm, chosen by the state and paid for by Central Hudson shareholders (absolutely not 
11 
with tax dollars, nor by ratepayers), needs to examine every account and determine how much 
12 
people have been overcharged. This is especially important, because no one– not the state, not 
13 
Central Hudson, not consumer advocates– knows exactly who has been overcharged and who 
14 
hasn’t. If it’s impossible to determine how much energy a household actually has used (which 
15 
could likely be the case since Central Hudson is a negligent utility company that’s failing to 
16 
fulfill the most basic requirements of utility company operations), then ratepayers should be 
17 
reimbursed based on a formula derived from usage rates and arrears in their area. Exactly who 
18 
was overbilled (and by how much) needs to be determined, and then reparations must be made, 
19 
not on the backs of its ratepayers, but by Central Hudson and its owner, Fortis, Inc. 
20 
 
21 
 
2 Order Adopting Terms of Joint Proposal and Establishing Electric and Gas Rate Plan, Case  20-E-0428/20-G-

Q-9.  Do you believe that there are households or businesses that have been 
1 
overcharged and are unaware of it? 
2 
A-9.  Yes, that’s precisely what I’m saying. I can tell you first hand, that even our 
3 
organization was overcharged over the course of the pandemic. We didn’t know it until a year or 
4 
two later; it was before I stepped into my role. We experienced inconsistent billing for numerous 
5 
months, receiving several bills each month with no clear indication of which was the correct one. 
6 
And, it turns out Central Hudson was also billing us way beyond our usage. It was taken out of 
7 
our autopay, each month, for at least a year (See Exhibit__JM-02).  
8 
 
9 
Q-10.  What do you mean by “beyond your usage”? 
10 
A-10.  Our office went unused for 1-2 years during the pandemic. The office doesn’t 
11 
actually have heat, and no lights were on. There was no one using it; it was unoccupied. Yet, on 
12 
several occasions, we were charged up to $300 monthly when our average bill is $100-$150. It 
13 
was taken out of our accounts with autopay. Had we been billed accurately, we would have had a 
14 
small amount each month for the standard delivery rates/cost of keeping our account open. I 
15 
know this, because I’m a landlord down the street and have 3 other accounts with Central 
16 
Hudson.  
17 
 
18 
Q-11.  Did you experience billing issues with those accounts? 
19 
A-11.  I’m not sure. I’ve always been in arrears, because I’ve never trusted Central 
20 
Hudson’s billing, even before they switched to their new system. I didn’t receive any wildly 
21 
inaccurate bills like so many others in Central Hudson territory have. However, I’m definitely 
22 
experiencing a billing issue now! My roommate switched our electric to her name, and the ability 
23 
3689

CASE NO. 23-E-0418/23-G-041 
 
9 
to pay my NYSERDA on-bill loan for the Assisted Home Performance with ENERGY STAR

2 
which processes the loan payments, and they said I must pay it through my Central Hudson 
3 
account. However, Central Hudson said that account no longer exists, and that I have to pay it to 
4 
the loan company, Concord. I can’t access the account online, because Central Hudson has 
5 
completely terminated it, as if it never existed.  
6 
 
7 
Q-12.  What is going to happen as a result of these billing issues?  
8 
A-12.  Concord said they will send it to collections if I don’t pay, and that I have to pay 
9 
through Central Hudson. Central Hudson says I have no account when I call them; however, they 
10 
have sent me one or two notices that I still owe them for the old account, with the old account 
11 
number. Because remember– I always keep a bit of arrears. So, when they closed my account, I 
12 
still owed them, in addition to the multi-thousand-dollar loan I had for NYSERDA’s Assisted 
13 
Home Performance with ENERGY STAR program. So, maybe I can get them to reopen my 
14 
account or find it or something, since they clearly have a record of it. Or perhaps my credit will 
15 
go down the drain, as a result of Central Hudson's incompetence at retaining basic things like 
16 
records from an account closed a month prior, or their lack of proper communication protocols 
17 
for an account being closed by someone who isn’t the account holder. 
18 
 
19 
Q-13.  What was your NYSERDA loan for? 
20 
A-13.  It was for an energy efficient oil burner. When I bought my home around 2015, 
21 
the boiler was faulty. I was in a real bind, and did the NYSERDA program so I could get an 
22 
efficient boiler, vs a standard one. I’m not sure they offer that option anymore, since the aim is to 
23

get off fossil fuels. I do see that Central Hudson on-bill loan recovery3 is still an option, which I

in a situation like me.

IV. Communities for Local Power and Energy Affordability 
5 
Q-14.  Does your organization work on energy affordability issues?  
6 
A-14.  Yes, in a variety of ways. At the local/grassroots level, Communities for Local 
7 
Power hosted seven debt relief workshops, with varying collaborators at each – PULP, local 
8 
allies, Department of Public Service, and social service agencies administering HEAP and 
9 
SNAP, at which we enrolled customers in the Energy Affordability Program (EAP), thus 
10 
qualifying them under Phase 1 of the Public Service Commission’s (PSC) electric/gas bill debt 
11 
cancellation program, for forgiveness of their energy arrears accrued prior to May 1, 2021. We 
12 
held 2 events in Ellenville– one at the senior center, during the “cafe” hours, and one at Family 
13 
of Ellenville, which provides various services and programs for low-income households, 
14 
domestic abuse victims, homeless individuals/families, and more. Additionally, we held events at 
15 
the Newburgh Public Library (in a very impoverished city in Orange County), at a food bank in a 
16 
rural Greene County community, at the YMCA in Kingston, and an online workshop geared 
17 
towards social workers and aid workers. 
18 
At the state/regulatory level, Communities for Local Power also pushes for debt relief 
19 
and advocates for ratepayers, especially for those who are low-income.  When the PSC’s Phase 2 
20 
Order of energy debt relief was released on January 19, 2023, it ordered New York utilities to 
21 
 
3 NYSERDA. “Residential Financing Programs.” https://www.nyserda.ny.gov/All-Programs/Residential-Financing-
Programs  
3691

CASE NO. 23-E-0418/23-G-041 
 
11 
allocate $672 million to pay off a portion of past-due utility bills for 478,000 non-Energy 
1 
Affordability Program-enrolled residential households and 56,000 small businesses.4  
2 
Communities for Local Power and other Parties united to demand increased shareholder 
3 
contributions by the Joint Utilities, but in the end only 12% of the cost was borne by utilities, 
4 
while ratepayers will pay for the other 88% via a monthly stipend for up to 10 years. In Phase 2, 
5 
individual utilities provided different percentages and amounts of debt relief, to be paid for by 
6 
differing charges to ratepayers.  
7 
Phase 3 of energy debt relief intends to work on ways to further automate and improve 
8 
the enrollment of qualifying ratepayers in EAP programs, and it will propose a plan for spending 
9 
a new $200 million allocation from the New York State Legislature, which was decided with last 
10 
year's budget. Communities for Local Power is working with a group of allied intervenor 
11 
organizations to press for more fair and equitable solutions. Communities for Local Power’s 
12 
Vice-President attends bi-weekly meetings of the energy affordability working group, which is 
13 
made up of people from the PSC, DPS, utilities across the state, plus advocates like the 
14 
intervenors mentioned above. The Working Group’s priorities were: (1) monitoring Phases 1 and 
15 
2 of Public Service Commission’s (Commission) arrears relief programs; (2) reviewing ways to 
16 
identify low-income customers and have them enroll into EAP; (3) reviewing utility and 
17 
stakeholder EAP Outreach and Education (O&E) plans; (4) measuring the energy burden for 
18 
EAP participants in the last program year; (5) monitoring a Low-Income Energy Bill and Usage 
19 
Study being conducted by NYSERDA; and (6) beginning work on the 2023-2024 New York 
20

Q-15.  Utilities have been tasked with helping implement energy affordability 
7 
programs for low-income households. Based on your knowledge, experience, and 
8 
observation, do you think they’re doing an adequate job? 
9 
A-15.  I can’t speak to other utilities, but I do not see Central Hudson’s contributions to 
10 
EAP enrollment as sufficient, nor effective. 
11 
 
12 
Q-16.  Why do you believe that their contributions are not sufficient or effective?  
13 
A-16.  Well, if they put just a fraction of their energy and marketing dollars they use for 
14 
promoting gas, I’d feel otherwise. But really – they don’t make it easy to enroll – people we were 
15 
helping didn’t really know about the program. Once you’re enrolled, you get un-enrolled pretty 
16 
quickly. I can tell you that from firsthand experience.  
17 
Q-17.  Can you share more about your experience being enrolled in the Energy 
18 
Affordability Program?  
19

Program, I learned about the qualifications required to enroll. In one of my apartments resides a

Q-18.  Based on your knowledge, experience, and observation, how might an 
17 
increase in Central Hudson rates affect landlords like yourself, with low-income residents? 
18 
A-18.  If Central Hudson rates increase, this rate case may directly result in hordes of 
19 
newly unhoused families living on the streets and in their cars. Landlords, like myself, who pay 
20 
for electric or gas, will need to increase the rent they charge. ALICE households (Asset Limited,

Income Constrained, Employed)6 a rapidly growing number of households who work, often 
1

2

3

4

V. Heat Pumps and Gas Promotion 
9 
Q-19.  With New York State setting targets for the purpose of increasing the 
10 
number of households to switch from gas and oil heat to electric heat (heat pumps), and to 
11 
increase electrical vehicle purchases and usage, based on your knowledge, experience, or 
12 
observation, do you believe households and businesses you work with will be inclined to 
13 
transition to electric cars and heat, with Central Hudson as their utility provider?  
14 
A-19.  I wish I could say yes. I wish I could say of course Central Hudson cares about 
15 
the climate crisis and New York’s efforts to combat it, so they’re making it a priority to have 
16 
ratepayers transition off fossil fuels. But my answer is no. Central Hudson ratepayers will be 
17 
very uninclined to switch to electric cars, and electric heat. We have zero trust in our utility. Why 
18 
would I switch from oil to a heat pump right now? I use an oil company that bills me on time, 
19 
with a bill that’s easy to understand, and it has the correct amount. If I have questions, I can call 
20 
the oil company and talk to a person who is an office a few streets down. We know from the 
21 
 
6 United For Alice, “Meet Alice.” https://www.unitedforalice.org/meet-alice 
3695


--- Page 434 ---
CASE NO. 23-E-0418/23-G-041 
 
15 
hearings that’s a drastic difference in dealing with Central Hudson. And why in the world would 
1 
I go through the time, effort, and cost of switching from oil heat to a heat pump, and risk getting 
2 
overbilled for each month? Plus – Central Hudson is still pushing gas on people, anyway. They 
3 
don’t care about people switching to heat pumps. They want everyone to switch to gas. 
4 
 
5 
Q-20.  What makes you argue that Central Hudson is pushing gas on ratepayers?  
6 
A-20.  A number of factors; their advertising, the mail they send, their website. So much 
7 
of it is about switching to natural gas. Meanwhile, part of the negotiations from their last rate 
8 
case required them to specifically change their language about gas and the manner in which they 
9 
promote it. There was a list of modifications that Central Hudson was required to make to their 
10 
website, which can be found in Appendix CC of the 2020 Central Hudson Rate Cases Joint 
11 
Proposal. Many of the modifications have not been fulfilled by Central Hudson (See 
12 
Exhibit__JM-01). 
13 
Below, I explore various parts of Central Hudson’s website to see how they have fulfilled 
14 
these modifications. Screenshots of these areas of the website can be found in Exhibit__JM-06.  
15

VI. Concerns Regarding Responsibly Sourced Gas 
1 
Q-23.  Can you share more about your concerns when it comes to New York’s 
2 
ability to lower its emissions?  
3 
A-23.  I’m very disturbed by Central Hudson’s plan to use “Responsibly Sourced Gas” 
4 
or RSG as a way to reduce emissions. In various reports and testimonies submitted by Central 
5 
Hudson, they have expressed a reliance on using RSG as a way to meet climate goals and 
6 
mandates. It is an integral part of their plan.  
7 
  
In page 6 of its CHGE IMPACT GHG ASSESSMENT, Central Hudson states the 
8 
Company “has piloted a program to purchase responsibly sourced gas (RSG) from certified 
9 
providers who effectively reduce upstream methane leakage from approximately 0.45% to 
10 
0.079% (or approximately 71 kg of methane per 1,000 mcf of gas produced). This analysis 
11 
assumes that 25% of gas sales moving forward can be procured from RSG providers at no 
12 
additional cost to ratepayers.”8  
13 
On Page 8 of CHGE IMPACT GHG ASSESSMENT, for “Responsibly Sourced Gas 
14 
(RSG) Purchases,” Central Hudson states there will be a “Total CO2e reduction (MT) results 
15 
from CHGE Impact GHG Assessment:13,932,” specifically with the “Apportionment: CH Gas 
16 
Meters in DAC.”9 Central Hudson claims that “the benefits of reduced methane emissions 
17 
associated with natural gas accrue to all customers, but more specifically those with natural gas 
18 
service that utilize the cleaner product. This benefit was allocated proportionally based on the 
19 
 
8 Climate Leadership and Sustainability Panel Schedule of Exhibits, CLSP-1, “CGHE Impact CHG Assessment.” 
https://documents.dps.ny.gov/public/Common/ViewDoc.aspx?DocRefId={1079AD89-0000-C753-83D2-
07904DA8EBAF} 
9 Ibid.  
3701


--- Page 440 ---
CASE NO. 23-E-0418/23-G-041 
 
21 
number of gas customers within DACs.”  I have concerns about the methodology they use to 
1 
come to this conclusion. 
2 
This isn’t the only place that Central Hudson touts the ability for RSG to reduce 
3 
emissions. In 23-01662/23-E-0418 Climate Leadership and Sustainability Testimony (Panel: 
4 
Stacey A. Renner, Melissa A. D’Antonio, Karen R. Lo, and Mark S. Sclafani)10, Central Hudson 
5 
states “RSG results in an approximately 82% reduction in upstream production (wellhead) 
6 
emissions which is the primary source of natural gas emissions.”  
7 
In CLP-07, IR-044, Central Hudson states that “RSG is certified by an independent third 
8 
party, which requires monitoring of each site to ensure that high standards (including 
9 
performance, functionality, design, and materials) are met, and any methane leaks are stopped 
10 
promptly.” In CLP-07, IR-045, the Company states “Central Hudson requires the Seller of that 
11 
RSG product to provide a certificate from a recognized and accredited industry organization 
12 
certifying the classification of the natural gas.” However, that’s not possible, because there’s no 
13 
accrediting organization or oversight body for certifying the classification of natural gas. RSG, 
14 
sometimes referred to as “certified-gas” or “differentiated gas,” is not regulated.11 It’s an 
15 
unregulated industry, with no set standards, and no independent body to oversee the monitoring 
16 
(See report “US Gas Can’t Certify Away its Climate and Environmental Justice Problems: A 
17 
Case Study on MIQ and Exxon” from Exhibit__JM-08). There are no safeguards to prevent 
18 
inaccurate or biased reporting. There’s no universal standard, or measurement protocol. 
19 
 
10 Direct Testimony of the Climate Leadership and Sustainability Panel, Central Hudson, Case No. 23-E-0418/23-
G-041.https://documents.dps.ny.gov/public/Common/ViewDoc.aspx?DocRefId=%7B3079AD89-0000-CA56-
A957-CA8DED9017C1%7D  
11 Murphy, Erin. “New York utility regulator approves a first-of-its-kind certified gas pilot program. Now what?” 
Environmental Defense Fund. https://blogs.edf.org/energyexchange/2023/09/19/new-york-utility-regulator-
approves-a-first-of-its-kind-certified-gas-pilot-program-now-what/

Q-24.  Are RSG and certified gas being used in places other than New York? 
1 
A-24.  Yes – and beyond that, New York isn’t the only state being courted by the RSG 
2 
industry – companies are pushing for it to be included in state climate plans. Just a few days ago, 
3 
in Mid-November, RSG was excluded from Colorado’s Clean Heat Plan.12 The plan lays out a 
4 
range of programs to comply with a groundbreaking law that requires Colorado gas companies to 
5 
slash emissions 4% by 2025 and 22% by 2030, based on 2015 levels.13  There are a lot of 
6 
parallels between Colorado’s Clean Heat Act and New York’s CLCPA, including not just the 
7 
states’ intentions, but also the approach of utility companies, one of them being Xcel Energy in 
8 
Colorado. According to Chuck Kutscher, mechanical engineer and former center 
9 
director/researcher at National Renewable Energy Laboratory, “Building electrification is a 
10 
serious threat to the business models of utilities that distribute natural gas. Xcel Energy has 
11 
responded by: 1) Casting doubt about the ability of heat pumps to heat Colorado homes, and 2) 
12 
Promoting a Clean Heat Plan that continues to rely heavily on natural gas.”14 However, it 
13 
appears that Colorado is a few steps ahead of New York, since the Colorado Energy Office and 
14 
Colorado Department of Health and Environment's Air Pollution Control Division “concluded 
15 
that certified gas and carbon offsets do not meet the definition of clean heat resources and 
16 
therefore the PUC should determine that Xcel cannot use them to achieve the 2030 target.”15 
17 
 
12 Brasch, Sam. “In a reversal, Xcel Energy won’t use carbon offsets or certified natural gas to clean up its gas 
system,” CPR News. https://www.cpr.org/2023/11/17/xcel-wont-use-carbon-offsets-certified-natural-gas-to-clean-
gas-system/

Q-25.  What are ways the Public Service Commission can be brought up to speed 
1 
regarding RSG, so that they can be more informed when creating CLCPA-aligned policy 
2 
and determining legitimate ratepayer costs? 
3 
A-25.  It’s imperative that the PSC, along with any other state energy policy decision 
4 
makers, look to unbiased resources and research that accurately portrays what RSG/certified-gas 
5 
is, as opposed to accepting utilities’ claims of what it is. It’s important the state not use wishful 
6 
thinking here; ignorance is not bliss. If Central Hudson or any other utilities intend to use RSG as 
7 
a way to decrease emissions, then the PSC must gather more information from independent 
8 
outside sources, not those affiliated with the actual certification firms, before accepting RSG as a 
9 
legitimate climate solution. I think it’s also worth pointing out that in Colorado, Xcel had the 
10 
opportunity to illustrate how and why the certified gas they wanted to use could meet the Clean 
11 
Heat Plan. Instead, they declined. It was their chance to show the world, and prove to the PUC 
12 
that their certified gas was worth having in the plan. Instead, they pulled out before the 
13 
evidentiary hearing. 
14 
 
15 
Q-26.  Why do you think Xcel in Colorado did not bring the issue of RSG to an 
16

17 
A-26.  I suspect they didn’t want to go to an evidentiary hearing because claims about 
18 
certified gas are unfounded. Wouldn't you want to go to the evidentiary hearing to show off your 
19 
technology? But their claims would be proven wrong – the proof isn’t in the pudding. And if that 
20 
3704

1

VII. Conclusions 
4 
Q-27.  Based on your knowledge, experience, and observation, is Central Hudson 
5 
falling short on fulfilling any other terms of the last rate case? 
6 
A-27.  Yes. During the last rate case, Communities for Local Power worked hard to have 
7 
Central Hudson provide Spanish language access on things like billing and their website. It’s 
8 
been years, and those terms of the agreement, available in Appendix Z, have not been met.  In 
9 
CLP-01, IR-06, Central Hudson even states “The bill is not currently issued in Spanish,” which 
10 
is one of the most integral parts of Spanish language access. In addition, their Spanish language 
11 
website (which is not utilized by most Spanish speakers in our community) is difficult to access 
12 
and riddled with language errors. They have not fulfilled the terms of their agreement since the 
13 
last rate case.  
14 
 
15 
Q-28.  How do you know Central Hudson has fallen short on fulfilling Spanish 
16 
language access?  
17 
A-28.  By using the website, and from the lack of bills being issued in Spanish. 
18 
 
19 
Q-29.  Based on your knowledge, experience, and observation, what do you consider 
20 
to be next steps in this rate case, and with Central Hudson? 
21 
 
16  Xcel Energy, “Select a Service Area to Explore.” https://my.xcelenergy.com/s/state-selector?return=%2Fs%2F

A-29.  Central Hudson is harming its ratepayers, and it's harming New York State. I 
1 
believe that the process of a public takeover of the company should start now, prompted by 
2 
what’s being revealed with this rate case, and with whatever else is being uncovered by the 
3

4

5

7
