Q-30.  What would a public takeover entail?  
11 
A-30.  A ton of work, that’s for sure! However, all of the things we do as advocates and 
12 
decisionmakers, such as this rate case, the CLCPA, the scoping plan, the DAC map, the 
13 
NYSERDA Energy Hubs… all of these things have taken a ton of work. We’re chugging along 
14 
though. Publicly-owned utilities exist – some of them function great, others don’t. There’s a lot 
15 
of research that needs to be done, but there’s good examples out there to emulate. It’s not 
16 
necessarily reinventing the wheel. We can look at the wheels out there, see what works well, 
17 
what doesn’t, and customize our own. We know that the Central Hudson model isn’t working – it 
18 
was even rated second to last in the 2022 JD Power Electric Utility Residential Customer 
19 
Satisfaction Study (See Exhibit__JM-10). But there’s some top-rated companies there too, that 
20 
are publicly-owned. We should see what they’re doing.  
21 
In addition, a public power takeover isn’t unheard of in New York. I’m from Long Island, 
22 
originally, and grew up amidst the Long Island Lighting Company (LILCO) public takeover by 
23 
3706


--- Page 445 ---
CASE NO. 23-E-0418/23-G-041 
 
26 
Long Island Power Authority (LIPA).17 It was a long process, but what isn’t? I’ll tell you one 
1 
thing – my parents who still live there would be up in arms if they were dealing with what we are 
2 
here in the Hudson Valley. Even LILCO’s transgressions pale in comparison to Central 
3 
Hudson’s! And while LIPA is far from perfect, it’s both better than Central Hudson, and a model 
4 
we can learn from. 
5 
 
6 
Q-31.  Based on your knowledge, experience, and observation, do you believe the 
7 
proposed rate increases are appropriate at this time? If not, please explain. 
8 
A-31.  No. I do not believe that rate increases are appropriate at this time. Instead, the 
9 
PSC must continue to deeply investigate Central Hudson’s negligent businesses practices. 
10 
Central Hudson must pay (without ratepayer funds) for all household and business accounts to be 
11 
audited by an independent firm approved by the state, so it can be determined what accounts 
12 
were overbilled, and by how much. Those funds need to be paid back to ratepayers. And the state 
13 
should begin the long process of a public takeover of Central Hudson, at the conclusion of this 
14 
rate case.

1 
issue. I have also provided written and oral comments to the EPA on the subject of greenhouse 
2 
gas emissions, monitoring technologies, methodologies, and associated regulations.

4 
II. Purpose of Testimony 
5 
Q-8.  What is the purpose of your testimony? 
6 
A-8.  We are providing testimony to share pertinent information from research relevant 
7 
to RSG rate cases. We urge the Public Service Commission to reject any proposed rate increases 
8 
including certified gas, responsibly sourced gas, differentiated gas, or so called optimized gas 
9 
with lower emissions. Our thorough and published research into gas certification programs (or 
10 
other similar schemes) shows that none of these programs has the capability to guarantee lower 
11 
emissions that would justify passing on higher prices to consumers (the full report is included as 
12 
Exhibit__JEDR-02). Their inability to guarantee lower emissions stems from four important 
13 
details.  
14 
The first detail is that monitors in the field consistently fail to operate and capture 
15 
emissions. Our research shows that for multiple reasons - including operator error, mechanical 
16 
error, and technological failures - monitors used by certifiers like Project Canary consistently 
17 
failed to capture pollution events.   
18 
The second detail is that there is no body that regulates or verifies that RSG certification 
19 
programs are what they claim they are or that emissions measurements are accurate and 
20 
substantiated.  
21 
The third detail is that Project Canary has no independent and peer reviewed studies to 
22 
substantiate the claims that their monitors consistently work as described in the field or, when 
23 
3712


--- Page 451 ---
CASE NO. 23-E-0418/23-G-0419 
 
 
5 
 
working, can capture acute but prolonged emissions events that make up a substantial portion of 
1 
emissions throughout the  oil and gas supply chain.   
2 
The fourth and final detail is that while certifiers like Project Canary claim they have 
3 
independent studies (not peer reviewed) to show the efficacy of their products those studies vary 
4 
from how we have observed monitor set up and design in the field. For instance, at one site in 
5 
our study Canary monitors were placed an average of 551 ft away from the closest source of 
6 
pollution (see figure 5.16 from “Certified Disasters” Exhibit__JEDR-02). However, in the study 
7 
often cited by Project Canary (see Exhibit JEDR-01), sensors were placed between 69 and 230 
8 
feet from sources. 
9 
Similarly the studies they self published and self funded monitoring systems included 8 
10 
monitors. However in the field we observed no more than 4 monitors placed around oil and gas 
11 
production sites. Often these monitors were placed with clear barriers between the monitors and 
12 
emissions sources. For example, during drilling and hydraulic fracturing we observed that 
13 
Project Canary and other monitors were consistently placed outside of 60 ft sound walls (see 
14 
figure 5.5 from “Certified Disasters” Exhibit__JEDR-02) 
15 
This lack of attention to detail (intentional or unintentional), oversight, and substantive 
16 
illustrates the inability to provide verifiable evidence of lower emissions. There are too many 
17 
unanswered questions and unverifiable data points and no regulatory body to hold them to 
18 
account for it all.  
19

III. Responsibly Sourced Gas and Certification 
5 
Q-10.  Based on your knowledge, experience, or observation, what does the term 
6 
Responsibly Sourced Gas (RSG) refer to, and are there any other terms that are 
7 
synonymous with it? 
8 
A-10.  There is no one definition for RSG. There are no defined levels of 
9 
“responsibility.” Project Canary’s Trustwell certification has no government endorsements, in 
10 
fact, we have only heard serious doubts about the platform's integrity from former and current 
11 
government officials as well as former employees of the company. One former employee, who 
12 
wishes to remain anonymous for safety concerns, told our research team that companies could 
13 
only receive the highest level (Platinum) certification if they agreed to purchase Project Canary 
14 
monitors. I’m not sure what that has to do with responsibility.  
15 
The term has been blurred and watered down to mean very little. And it is important to 
16 
remember that operators pay companies like Project Canary and MiQ to put their stamp of 
17 
approval on the gas. Other names that could be synonymous with RSG are certified gas, 
18 
differentiated gas, next gen gas, or so-called optimized gas. There may be others as well. 
19 
 
20 
Q-11.  New York’s CLCPA has the specific goal of a zero-emission electricity sector 
21 
by 2040, and sets targeted emissions reduction standards to get New York completely off of 
22 
3715


--- Page 454 ---
CASE NO. 23-E-0418/23-G-0419 
 
 
8 
 
fossil fuels by 2050. In your professional opinion, do you consider RSG as an appropriate 
1 
pathway for NY to fulfill its emission requirements? 
2 
A-11.  Josh Eisenfeld: I do not. Our report suggests that RSG certification schemes are 
3 
more likely to provide oil and gas companies with justification to increase production than they 
4 
are to reduce absolute emissions.  
5 
The reason being that emissions reduction potential is still unverified (as outlined above) 
6 
yet already we are seeing certifications used to sell increasingly more gas that is being marketed 
7 
as ‘clean.’ 
8 
Dr. Dakota Raynes: I do not. Do you remember the slogan “natural gas is a bridge fuel” 
9 
(brought to us in the 1990s by the likes of Enron)? Here we are, approximately 30 years later – 
10 
apparently gas was a bridge fuel to more gas – the new buzz words are differentiated gas, 
11 
certified gas, “responsibly sourced gas,” next gen gas, optimized gas, etc.  
12 
Meanwhile, the production, storage, transportation, distribution, and end-consumer use of 
13 
gas – differentiated, certified, “responsibly sourced,” or otherwise – is not currently capable of 
14 
being zero-emission and even proponents of new advancements in technology (such as 
15 
continuous emissions monitors or carbon capture storage and utilization) are uncertain of the 
16 
efficacy of these innovations to reduce emissions, nevermind eliminate them.   
17 
 
18 
Q-12.  Based on your knowledge, experience, or observation, who decides what the 
19 
standards for certification will be, and how is certification data verified?  
20 
A-12.  The emerging field of methane measurement and gas rating or certification is 
21 
young, there are many players experimenting with a myriad of techniques. Notably, a recent 
22 
report likened the current gas certification market to “a Wild West frontier filled with 
23 
3716


--- Page 455 ---
CASE NO. 23-E-0418/23-G-0419 
 
 
9 
 
mudslinging and smoke and mirrors – and the gunslingers are deciding the rules as they go 
1 
along”2 (see Exhibit_JEDR-03). 
2 
This is in part because there is little consensus in the tech industry, oil and gas industry, 
3 
research literature, and regulatory structures regarding what to call the increasing variety of types 
4 
of monitors, associated software and data analysis techniques, rating or certification program 
5 
standards, or even the gas that is produced and sold.  
6 
Currently there is no overarching regulatory body that defines or operationalizes any of 
7 
the key terms, technologies, methodologies, standards, ratings, or certifications - and there is no 
8 
clearinghouse to which such emissions data is submitted so that it can be truly independently 
9 
validated and verified. This means that each company involved in rating or certification gets to 
10 
create their own language, definitions, policies, procedures, practices, standards, and 
11 
certifications without regulatory oversight, validation, or verification of any part of the process 
12 
or final product. In other words, on the whole, gas rating and certification is a black box.  
13 
This situation creates a host of problems such as: 
14 
1. the ability for operators to cherry pick which rating or certification process they 
15 
want to use 
16 
2. the ability for operators to cherry pick which facilities are included or excluded in 
17 
a rating or certification process, resulting in unrepresentative samples and 
18 
potentially misleading statements about emissions profiles  
19 
3. the ability for rating and certification companies to change any part of the process 
20 
at any point in time 
21 
 
2 Ball, Jeffrey. “Inside the high-dollar race to sell natural gas as low-carbon” 
https://www.canarymedia.com/articles/liquefied-natural-gas/inside-the-high-dollar-race-to-sell-natural-gas-as-low-
carbon 
3717


--- Page 456 ---
CASE NO. 23-E-0418/23-G-0419 
 
 
10 
 
4. the ability for rating and certification companies to promulgate use of 
1 
technologies and methodologies that have not been proven fit-for-purpose in peer-
2 
reviewed experiments 
3 
5. an inability for consumers of rated or certified gas to verify and know that they 
4 
are getting what they are paying for 
5 
6. an inability for regulators, researchers, watchdogs, and the general public to be 
6 
able to fully understand gas rating and certification schemes and make informed 
7 
decisions, draft effective regulations, or effectively implement climate policy 
8 
Our Certified Disasters report makes a number of recommendations for improvements in 
9 
rated or certified gas schemes - three of them are quite relevant here: 
10 
1. rating or certification programs must be paired with accredited transition 
11 
pathways towards a managed decline of fossil fuels 
12 
2. key terms need explicit and transparent standardized definitions that are supported 
13 
by underlying data (e.g. “low methane intensity gas” needs to both have a defined 
14 
range or threshold and gas labeled as such needs to be empirically proven to be in 
15 
that range or below that threshold) 
16 
3. technologies and methodologies used in rating or certification programs need to 
17 
be proven fit-for-purpose via independent, peer-reviewed experimental analyses 
18 
that reflect real world conditions and these data and findings must be made 
19 
publicly available  
20

Q-13.  Can you share information about the research project that you conducted at 
1 
well sites? What did your team find while surveying the gas operations being monitored by 
2 
these certification companies?  
3 
A-13.  Josh Eisenfeld: Before explaining our exact methodology, it might be best to 
4 
understand the reason we conducted the study. For roughly 1 year, starting in early 2021, I 
5 
dedicated a portion of my time to gathering information about project canary’s continuous 
6 
emissions monitoring. As an employee of an NGO which aims to protect communities from the 
7 
impacts of extractive industries, I found the idea of cheap monitors paired with computer 
8 
technology to be an exciting opportunity. However, the excitement was curbed with the realistic 
9 
expectations that this tech still needed to be tested in field conditions by independent parties. I 
10 
thought one way to do this would be for Earthworks to use our FLIR cameras to look for leaks at 
11 
sites with Project Canary monitors then compare what we found with what they found.  
12 
Earthworks’ Field Advocates use industry-standard FLIR GF320 Optical Gas Imaging 
13 
(OGI) cameras designed to detect and visualize 20 volatile organic compounds that cannot be 
14 
detected by the naked eye, including the carcinogens benzene and toluene as well as methane. 
15 
Earthworks’ Field Advocates are ITC (Infrared Training Center) certified optical gas imaging 
16 
thermographers. The cameras are routinely calibrated by FLIR in accordance with their standards 
17 
for accurate recording.  
18 
Unfortunately - and despite Project Canary’s claims of radical transparency - Project 
19 
Canary publishes no monitoring data. They claim that it is not their decisions but their clients 
20 
and I will respect that may be true. Regardless, it is not the transparency I had expected.  
21 
Then, in 2021, the Colorado Department of Public Health and Environment (CDPHE) 
22 
adopted new permitting requirements for all new oil and gas wells under Regulation 7 Part D 
23 
3719


--- Page 458 ---
CASE NO. 23-E-0418/23-G-0419 
 
 
12 
 
VI.C (referred to as “Regulation 7”), which states: “Owners or operators of drilling operations 
1 
that begin on or after May 1, 2021, must monitor air quality at and/or around the pre-production 
2 
and early production operations.”3 The regulation requires that operators use continuous 
3 
emissions monitors (CEMs), like Project Canary’s Canary-S monitor, to either monitor methane 
4 
directly or through known precursors like Volatile Organic Compounds (VOCs) such as 
5 
Benzyne, Toluene, Ethylbenzene, and Xylene (BTEX). 
6 
It also requires operators to create an Air Quality Monitoring Plan (AQMP) to provide 
7 
technical details about what monitors will be used, how they will be deployed, what pollution 
8 
they will detect, and what thresholds define an event that requires operator response. 
9 
Additionally, operators must submit monthly reports, including hourly monitoring data (averages 
10 
of 60 one-minute readings), equipment failures, and the total number of pollution events 
11 
requiring action for that month.  
12 
Such regulations create a baseline standard of reporting that can then be tested and 
13 
verified using other technologies to ensure that companies are living up to the ideals they are 
14 
promoting. Documents associated with regulations at the local, state, and federal levels are often 
15 
publicly available upon request. This includes permit applications, monitoring reports, and 
16 
notices of violations, to name just a few.  
17 
While studies show CEMs can be reliable in controlled test settings, these tests represent 
18 
a significant simplification of field conditions4 (See Exhibit__JEDR-04). Comparing Earthworks 
19 
field observations with data reported to the CDPHE under Regulation 7 provides an opportunity 
20 
to gain insight into the practical capabilities of CEMs. 
21 
 
3 5 CCR § 1001-9 Part D VI.C.  
https://www.sos.state.co.us/CCR/GenerateRulePdf.do?ruleVersionId=9113&fileName=5%20CCR%201001-9  
4 Bell, C. et al., “Performance of Continuous Emission Monitoring Solutions Under Single-Blind Controlled Testing

13 
 
Over a period of 7 months in 2022 (May-November), Earthworks’ certified 
1 
thermographers conducted a total of 77 surveys of 30 different oil and gas production sites in the 
2 
Front Range where Photoionization Detector (PID) based CEMs were deployed. These sites 
3 
were selected because they had recently been permitted and therefore were part of the CDPHE 
4 
Regulation 7 program. Because Earthworks field advocates spend the majority of their time 
5 
helping communities document issues from oil and gas sites across the state, we limited our 
6 
visits to sites based on factors such as convenience of access, proximity to others we had already 
7 
planned to visit for community complaints, and certainty that the wells had valid monitoring 
8 
plans and monitor types were known. These same factors also played into whether or not we 
9 
returned to a site again.  
10 
The breakdown of monitors is as follows: 21 of these sites used Project Canary Canary-S 
11 
monitors, six used SENSIT SPOD monitors, two used Aeroqual AQS-1 monitors, and one used 
12 
SGS Smart Sense monitors. Earthworks recorded 22 pollution events (14 at sites with Project 
13 
Canary-S monitors, five at sites with SENSE-IT SPOD monitors, two at sites with Aeroqual 
14 
AQS-1 monitors, and one at a site with SGS SmartSense monitors) from a wide variety of well 
15 
site activities spanning production phases – including emissions from drilling, fracking, flaring, 
16 
venting, and maintenance. The team then verified that footage with independent industry 
17 
professionals and, when necessary, submitted that footage as an official complaint to the 
18 
CDPHE.  
19 
In response to these observations, CDPHE shared Earthworks’ footage with operators and 
20 
requested that they investigate and identify any air quality compliance issues. In all cases, 
21 
operators claimed they could not identify any issues or noncompliance and, in some instances, 
22 
cited their CEM readings to reinforce these claims. When operators provided CDPHE with CEM 
23 
3721


--- Page 460 ---
CASE NO. 23-E-0418/23-G-0419 
 
 
14 
 
readings as evidence, Earthworks documented how those data sets further confirmed CEM 
1 
failures. 
2 
There were some limitations to what our data shows that I want to make clear. Due to the 
3 
limitations of Regulation 7, the research team does not know precise monitor readings at the 
4 
exact moment Earthworks documented the pollution event. Regulation 7 only requires operators 
5 
to provide an hourly aggregate reading for each metric they record rather than the minute-by-
6 
minute data that should come from CEMs.  
7 
Regulation 7 allows operators flexibility in which pollutants and metrics they record. 
8 
However, it requires at least one of the following: “total VOCs, methane, benzene or BTEX 
9 
(benzene, toluene, ethylbenzene, and xylenes) or other indicators of hydrocarbon emissions from 
10 
pre-production and early production operations.’’ Although there was some variation in which 
11 
metrics were recorded from site to site and even from month to month, all of the sites in this 
12 
study included total VOC (tVOC) emissions readings from their monitors. This metric also 
13 
worked well for comparison with the Earthworks OGI camera, which also does not specify the 
14 
different hydrocarbon emissions it detects. 
15 
Operators were also required to define response levels for the pollutants they monitor and 
16 
the actions that would be taken if elevated levels were exceeded. Earthworks researchers chose 
17 
to use these operator-defined thresholds as one of the criteria to define what was or was not a 
18 
pollution event.  
19 
As monitoring reports became publicly available at the end of each month, Earthworks 
20 
filed records requests with CDPHE. If pollution was detected with Earthworks OGI cameras, the 
21 
team recorded it for at least 10 minutes. After recording, the team analyzed the video footage 
22 
against well pad maps and other records operators filed with CDPHE to determine which CEMs 
23 
3722


--- Page 461 ---
CASE NO. 23-E-0418/23-G-0419 
 
 
15 
 
on site would most likely detect the pollution event. The team then reviewed the corresponding 
1 
monthly monitoring report to determine if CEMs captured evidence of the same pollution event. 
2 
For CEMs, Earthworks defined a pollution event as monitor readings that exceeded the 
3 
operator’s “Response Level Event” as defined in their AQMP or if any of the monitors at the 
4 
time of OGI documented pollution reported a “Maximum” or “Mean” level more than four times 
5 
the monthly average at that site. If CEM readings did not indicate a pollution event as defined 
6 
above, it was counted as a failure to detect pollution. Notably, Project Canary’s comments on the 
7 
EPA’s proposed supplemental rule regarding emissions guidelines for the oil and gas industry 
8 
also suggest the use of OGI inspection to verify that CEM systems are operating correctly5 (see 
9 
Exhibit JEDR-05).  
10 
 
11 
Q-14.  What type of pollution occurs at well sites? What’s the impact on adjacent 
12 
communities? 
13 
A-14.  Per the Energy Information Administration, the majority of US gas is produced 
14 
via hydraulic fracturing (approximately 80-97% depending on the year). Physicians for Social 
15 
Responsibility have been routinely collecting, summarizing, analyzing, and synthesizing the 
16 
ever-growing body of peer-reviewed research on the health impacts of fracking and associated 
17 
oil and gas infrastructure – last month they released the 9th edition of their compendium of this 
18 
research.6  
19 
Some of the key findings are: 
20 
 
5 Standards of Performance for New, Reconstructed, and Modified Sources and Emissions Guidelines for Existing Sources: Oil

1. Air pollution = “As documented in more than 110 studies, toxic air pollution 
1 
accompanies fracking. More than 200 airborne chemical contaminants have been 
2 
detected near drilling and fracking operations, and air monitoring has confirmed 
3 
strikingly high levels of toxic air pollutants in communities near these sites. Of 
4 
these chemicals, 61 are classified as hazardous air pollutants with known health 
5 
risks, including the potent carcinogens benzene and formaldehyde. Additional 
6 
fracking related air pollutants include diesel exhaust, fine particles, hydrogen 
7 
sulfide gas, nitrogen oxides, chlorine, and other chemical precursors of ground-
8 
level ozone (smog), which can damage respiratory, cardiovascular, and nervous 
9 
systems. In many cases, concentrations of fracking related air pollutants in 
10 
communities where people live and work exceed federal safety standards, even in 
11 
some rural communities. Research shows that air emissions from fracking and 
12 
flaring can drift and pollute the air hundreds of miles downwind. Indoor air 
13 
pollution from gas stoves, which represent the terminus of the fracking pipeline, 
14 
includes nitrogen oxides, carbon monoxide, fine particulate matter, and benzene. 
15 
About one in every eight cases of asthma in U.S. children is attributable to 
16 
exposure to emissions from gas stoves.” 
17 
2. Water scarcity and water pollution = “Each day in the United States, more than 
18 
two billion gallons of pressurized fracking fluids are pumped underground for the 
19 
purpose of extracting oil and gas or, after the fracking is finished, to inject the 
20 
extracted wastewater into any of more than 187,000 disposal wells across the 
21 
country. As documented by more than 200 studies, these fracking-related 
22 
activities have depleted or contaminated water resources, including drinking water 
23 
3724


--- Page 463 ---
CASE NO. 23-E-0418/23-G-0419 
 
 
17 
 
sources. Studies from across the United States provide irrefutable evidence that 
1 
groundwater contamination has occurred as a result of fracking activities and is 
2 
more likely to occur close to well pads. Spills and intentional discharges of 
3 
fracking fluids and fracking waste into surface water have profoundly altered the 
4 
chemistry and ecology of streams throughout entire watersheds, increasing 
5 
downstream levels of 10 radioactive elements, heavy metals, endocrine disruptors, 
6 
toxic disinfection byproducts, and acidity, and decreasing aquatic biodiversity. 
7 
Demand for water to use in U.S. fracking operations has more than doubled since 
8 
2016. The water used for fracking that remains in the shale formation is 
9 
permanently lost to the hydrological cycle. Studies also show that fracking can 
10 
deplete streams and aquifers in ways that create water scarcity in drought-prone 
11 
regions. Along with fracking itself, the injection of fracking waste is a proven 
12 
cause of earthquakes. The disposal of fracking wastewater remains a problem 
13 
with no solution.” 
14 
3. Public health concerns = “With more than 17.6 million U.S. residents living 
15 
within one mile of at least one active oil and gas well, the result is a public health 
16 
crisis. As documented in more than 120 studies, public health harms now linked 
17 
with drilling, fracking, and associated infrastructure are well established. They 
18 
include cancers, asthma, respiratory diseases, skin rashes, heart problems, and 
19 
mental health problems. Multiple corroborating studies of pregnant women 
20 
residing near fracking operations across the nation show impairments to infant 
21 
health, including birth defects, preterm birth, and low birth weight. Emerging 
22 
evidence shows harm to maternal health -  including elevated risks for eclampsia 
23 
3725


--- Page 464 ---
CASE NO. 23-E-0418/23-G-0419 
 
 
18 
 
during pregnancy - and shortened life spans among older residents living in 
1 
proximity to oil and gas wells.” 
2 
In addition to all of the problems mentioned above, oil and gas industry activities are 
3 
associated with a number of other harms that are also widely documented in peer-reviewed 
4 
research such as: increased noise and light pollution; increased traffic, traffic accidents, and 
5 
deterioration of infrastructure (roads, bridges, etc.); increased social conflict; increases in a 
6 
variety of types of crimes (most notably violent crimes such as assault and sexual assault); soil 
7 
contamination; disruption of farming and ranching activities; disruption of non-human animal 
8 
migration patterns; and, disruption of healthy ecosystem functionality.

Q-15.  Based on your knowledge, experience, or observation, are the claims that gas 
11

12 
A-15.  From the limited data they have publicly provided, it is impossible to say for sure. 
13 
What we have seen certainly indicates that they are not or at the very least, we must do more 
14 
research before we can rely on these technologies. Other, peer reviewed, studies support this 
15 
suggestion as well7 (See Exhibit__JEDR-06).

17 
Q-16.  On page 7 of the “Certified Disaster: How Project Canary & Gas 
18 
Certification are Misleading Markets & Governments” report, it’s stated that “numerous

certification.” Have those concerns been made public on their part, and if yes, to what

Q-17.  Can you explain the difference between “Alternative Continuous Monitoring 
18 
Systems” – such as those offered by Project Canary, and OGI leak detection? 
19 
A-17.  OGI cameras are powerful, handheld, scientific instruments that use infrared 
20 
thermal-imaging processes and spectral filters to visualize the presence of various hydrocarbon 
21 
and VOC emissions based on their unique electromagnetic radiation absorption patterns (or 
22 
wavelengths). This technology can detect the short, medium, or long wavelengths within the 
23 
3727


--- Page 466 ---
CASE NO. 23-E-0418/23-G-0419 
 
 
20 
 
infrared portion of the spectrum. An OGI operator can focus the camera on equipment of any 
1 
size to pinpoint the exact source of an emission.  
2 
Essentially, OGI cameras are specifically designed to take pollution that is invisible to the 
3 
naked eye and make it visible, recordable, and in some cases, measurable, all in real-time.  
4 
OGI cameras have been used to identify emissions associated with oil and gas industry 
5 
facilities and activities for nearly 20 years, they are a scientifically tested and proven method of 
6 
emissions detection, and they are both the industry and regulatory standard for best emissions 
7 
reduction. There are clear, widely agreed upon standards for calibration of OGI cameras as well 
8 
as training and certification of OGI operators (aka thermographers). Utilization of OGI cameras 
9 
outside of industry or regulatory settings to quantify emissions is a more recent development and 
10 
has yet to be adequately verified via independent or peer-reviewed analysis. 
11 
There is scant publicly available information about the specific types of monitors that 
12 
comprise different companies’ alternative continuous monitoring systems. What information 
13 
does exist suggests that many continuous emissions monitors employ relatively simple 
14 
photoionization detectors (consisting of a power source, an ultraviolet lamp, an ionization 
15 
chamber, electrodes, and a display or some other means of reporting data collected). They are 
16 
often small enough to be handheld and can be configured in various ways (e.g., different power 
17 
sources, lamp strengths, etc.) to best detect a specific gas. The ultraviolet lamp emits photons 
18 
that are absorbed by a gas inside an ionization chamber; the electrodes then collect the ions 
19 
produced in the process. The current produced in this process allows for measuring the gas in 
20 
question. 
21 
Essentially, PIDs are a piece of hardware designed to record ambient gas concentrations 
22 
(detection levels vary) in a specific place at certain intervals (every second, minute, hour, etc.). 
23 
3728


--- Page 467 ---
CASE NO. 23-E-0418/23-G-0419 
 
 
21 
 
Then, the software kicks in, and an algorithm translates each moment of data collected into a log 
1 
of emission events. These algorithms vary in complexity and efficacy, they can be built to take 
2 
into account several different considerations, such as wind speed, wind direction, temperature, 
3 
humidity, or background levels of a gas; they can use a variety of different dispersion models to 
4 
estimate the size and location of the emission; and, they can be set to create an alarm if a pre-set 
5 
threshold is exceeded. 
6 
Notably, even the Methane Emissions Technology Evaluation Center (part of Colorado 
7 
State University’s Energy Institute) has come to the conclusion that “continuous monitors should 
8 
be used with caution as “detection limits, probability of detection, localization, and 
9 
quantification may or may not be fit-for-purpose for any given application” and “relying on 
10 
quantification estimates from these solutions for emissions reporting is likely premature at this 
11 
point”8 (see Exhibit JEDR-04).  
12 
Likewise, other researchers examining the utility of CEMs also found that at this time, 
13 
accurate site-level or equipment-specific emission quantification “is still an open problem,”9 
14 
CEMs do not have reliable localization capabilities, and “it is unclear whether that can be 
15 
accomplished using low-cost sensors.”10 Furthermore, there is wide variability in emission 
16 
detection capacity relative to the size of emissions, a host of issues with various methods of 
17 
 
8 Bell, Clay, et al. “Performance of Continuous Emission Monitoring Solutions.” 
9 Jiayang Lyra Wang, et al., “Multiscale Methane Measurements at Oil and Gas Facilities Reveal Necessary 
Frameworks for Improved Emissions Accounting,” Environmental Science & Technology, 56, 20 (2022): 14743 
14752. https://doi.org/10.1021/acs.est.2c06211 
10 Torgrim Log, Wegar Bjerkeli Pedersen, and Heike Moumets “Optical Gas Imaging (OGI) as a Moderator for 
Interdisciplinary Cooperation, Reduced Emissions, and Increased Safety,” Energies 12, 8, (2019): 1454. 
https://doi.org/10.3390/en12081454 
3729


--- Page 468 ---
CASE NO. 23-E-0418/23-G-0419 
 
 
22 
 
plume analysis, and “instabilities in sensors [can] reduce periods when reliable information can 
1 
be collected.”11 
2 
Additionally, other studies bring attention to the effects topography, micrometeorological 
3 
conditions, basin-specific characteristics, and human error can have on results since CEMs need 
4 
to be appropriately placed on-site so that any emission plume has a “high probability of 
5 
intersecting one or more sensors, regardless of wind direction.”12 Determining the appropriate 
6 
placement is challenging, as emissions associated with oil and gas industry activities exhibit high 
7 
spatial and temporal variability. Yet, CEMs are fixed in one location, and changes in wind speed 
8 
and direction have a significant effect on detection capabilities.13 Sensor placement and 
9 
algorithm development need to be able to account for the complexity of the environment into 
10 
which they are deployed, including the type of equipment being monitored; the number of pieces 
11 
of equipment; the distance between monitors and equipment monitored; potential sources of 
12 
interference; the effects of humidity, water vapor, and wind; and, the effects of stagnation and 
13 
dilution14 (see Exhibit JEDR-08). Lastly, methane emissions vary considerably within and across 
14 
oil and gas basins,15 (see Exhibit__JEDR-07) and can evolve over time as infrastructure ages, 
15 
 
11 Stuart N. Riddick, et al., “The Calibration and Deployment of a LowCost Methane Sensor,” Atmospheric 
Environment, 230 (2020): 117440. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.atmosenv.2020.117440 
12 Eben D. Thoma, et al., “Facility Fence-Line Monitoring Using Passive Samplers,” Journal of the Air & Waste

new wells are drilled, or production declines,16 therefore “measurement approaches should be

Regardless of the type of technology used (with the exception of OGI), “most sensors and

Q-19.  To your knowledge, what there a regulatory body that oversees RSG? 
1 
A-19.  There is no regulatory body that oversees RSG or any other similar schemes. We 
2 
have undertaken multiple efforts to communicate with regulators about this issue but none have 
3 
claimed authority over auditing claims made by gas certification schemes. 
4 
What we have found is that, much like the early days of “organic foods” there is no 
5 
standard, there is no verification of practices, and therefore the claim has no meaning.

Q-20.  Based on your knowledge, experience, or observation, how easy would it be 
8 
for a company to manipulate the market, and provide RSG that’s certification is based on 
9 
the collection of biased data collection?  
10 
A-20.  This was the primary concern which led to the field study and subsequent 
11 
“Certified Disaster” report. There is no oversight and there is no independent peer reviewed 
12 
evidence to prove what certifiers like Project Canary are claiming is true. In fact there are only 
13 
studies that show serious doubts that their products work when leak rates are below 5 kg/hr. One 
14 
peer reviewed and published article, which looked at the efficacy of multiple continuous 
15 
monitors including the Project Canary Canary-X monitor, clearly states:  
16 
The large variability in performance between [continuous monitoring] solutions, coupled 
17 
with highly uncertain detection, detection limit, and quantification results, indicates that 
18 
the performance of individual [continuous monitoring] solutions should be well 
19 
understood before relying on results for internal emissions mitigation programs or 
20 
regulatory reporting.21 (see Exhibit__JEDR-04) 
21 
 
21 Bell, Clay, et al. “Performance of Continuous Emission Monitoring Solutions.”

This study also illustrates a clear example of how data can be easily manipulated. In Bell 
1 
et al (2023) - and in most (if not all) of the studies published on Project Canary’s website - 8 
2 
Canary-X monitors were used during experiments at the test site. However, our observations 
3 
suggest that in the field Project Canary never uses more than 4 monitors. According to Bell et al 
4 
(2023) using less monitors can have a direct impact on correctly identifying pollution: “5 of the 7 
5 
solutions with the highest [True Positive] rates deployed 6 or more sensors, and 3 of 4 solutions 
6 
with the lowest [True Positive] rates installed only 1 sensor” (See Exhibit__JEDR-04).  
7 
So, by using less monitors Project Canary has manipulated the data. 8 monitors is 
8 
different from 4 monitors - a lot different. We have never seen or heard any mention of this from 
9 
Project Canary. This is not the clear and transparent data that they claim to provide. And this is 
10 
just one example of many. The data is easily manipulated.

18

19

20 
alone, front-of-the-meter, battery energy storage systems greater than 5 MW intended to provide 
21

22

Based on Central Hudson’s discovery responses and publicly available information, it appears 
3 
that Central Hudson’s currently proposed rates will make it very difficult for bulk storage to be 
4 
economically developed in the Company’s service territory. That is particularly true because at 
5

6 
115 kV and potentially 345 kV1 interconnected bulk storage facilities in Central Hudson’s 
7

8

9 
These projects will pay state-jurisdictional rates but those that are 115kV or above are not 
10 
eligible to be part of the DER Utilization Earnings Adjustment Mechanism (“EAM”).5 In our 
11 
professional experience, most battery projects interconnected at those voltages are generally 
12 
federally-jurisdictional, and as a result, are not subject to these charges.6  If applied without

9 
 
III. Introduction to New York State Battery Storage Policy 
1 
Q. Why is battery storage policy important in the context of this rate case? 
2 
A. As discussed below, New York State has a stated policy of encouraging battery storage in 
3 
support of its renewable energy obligations under state law. The rates paid by battery storage for 
4 
its charging load are a substantial driver of project economics and constructability. As a result, 
5 
understanding state policy drivers is important context for understanding rate implications. 
6 
 
7 
Q. Please provide a summary of New York State energy storage policy as it pertains to your 
8 
testimony. 
9 
A. In 2018, the Commission established goals of 1,500 megawatts of energy storage capacity by 
10 
2025 and 3,000 megawatts by 2030, adopting the recommendations of the New York Energy 
11 
Storage Roadmap (Roadmap).7  In July 2019, the Community Leadership and Climate Protection 
12 
Act (“CLCPA”) was signed into law. The CLCPA established statewide greenhouse gas 
13 
emissions limits and renewable energy goals, including a renewable energy standard of seventy 
14 
percent renewable energy by 2030, and a standard of zero net electric emissions by 2040.8   
15 
Following recommendations of the CLCPA-established Climate Action Council, Governor 
16 
Hochul announced a new goal of 6 gigawatts of storage capacity statewide by 2030 to support 
17 
the State’s renewable energy needs, and directed the New York State Energy Research and 
18 
Development Authority (“NYSERDA”) and Department of Public Service (“DPS”) staff to 
19 
 
7 “Confirming Order” [Confirming New York State Energy Storage Roadmap and Department of Public Service/New 
York State Energy Research and Development Authority Staff Recommendations], In the Matter of Energy Storage 
Deployment Program, Case No. 18-E-0130, (July 13, 2018).  
8 New York state climate leadership and community protection act, Senate Bill 6599, 2019-2020 Legislative Session 
(signed July 18, 2019).  
3744


--- Page 483 ---
 
 
10 
 
update the Roadmap to meet the new target.9 On December 28, 2022, PSC and NYSERDA staff 
1 
filed an updated Energy Storage Roadmap with a plan to achieve the 6 gigawatt target with a 
2 
procurement of 3,000 megawatts of storage capacity under a new Index Storage Credit (“ISC”) 
3 
mechanism.10  
4 
 
5 
Q. What is the role of bulk storage in New York’s energy storage policy?  
6 
A. In its 2018 Order Establishing Energy Storage Goal and Deployment Policy,11 the 
7 
Commission approved multiple programs to support the deployment of bulk storage projects.  
8 
The Order authorized NYSERDA to spend $310 million plus associated administration and 
9 
program costs on a Market Acceleration Bridge Incentive (“MBI”) to energy storage projects at 
10 
the customer, distribution, and bulk level. NYSERDA awarded $115 million in incentives to 14 
11 
bulk storage projects comprising 550 megawatts of capacity, though few of those projects have 
12 
been built to date.12 The 2018 Order also directed electric Investor Owned Utilities (“IOUs”) to 
13 
issue RFPs to competitively procure dispatch rights for a combined 350 megawatts of bulk-level 
14 
energy storage systems.13  Additionally, Large-Scale Renewable REC Solicitations and Offshore

Wind Solicitations give additional consideration to bids that include energy storage projects in 
1 
combination with large-scale renewables projects to receive contracts.14

The Updated Energy Storage Roadmap (“Updated Roadmap”) proposes to rely on bulk storage 
3

Q. How will the Index Storage Credit mechanism support the deployment of bulk battery storage 
10 
systems?  
11 
A. In proposing the Index Storage Credit (“ISC”) mechanism, DPS and NYSERDA recognized 
12 
that a state program is necessary to incentivize battery storage projects. Due to the “missing 
13 
money” problem in wholesale markets, there are not sufficient wholesale market revenues to 
14 
incentivize construction of bulk storage facilities in New York State.16  Under the ISC, 
15 
NYSERDA will award long-term contracts to bulk storage projects through a competitive 
16 
solicitation.17   The program is predicated on the bulk energy storage project participating in 
17

4

5 
factors. It seems likely that ISC solicitations will award projects that have low costs and thus are 
6 
able to submit competitively priced bids.

IV: Central Hudson Rate Assignments for Bulk Storage Projects 
9 
Q. What Central Hudson rates will be applicable to bulk storage projects interconnected in the 
10 
Company’s service territory?  
11 
A.  Based on the Company’s current tariff and proposed rates in this proceeding, bulk storage 
12 
projects in Central Hudson’s service territory will be assigned to SC 13.18 The Company’s tariff 
13 
states that SC 13 is applicable to customers that provide all equipment necessary to take service 
14 
from the Company at voltages of 69 kV or higher. SC 13 has two rate options including: (1) a 
15 
Substation Rate and (2) a Transmission Rate. The Substation rate option applies to customers 
16 
taking service directly from a substation having a line-side voltage of 69 kV or higher. The 
17 
Transmission rate option is for customers taking service at voltages of 69 kV or higher19.20  The 
18 
Company refers to the “Character of Service” guidelines to determine which rate option would 
19 
apply and indicates that the rate option is based on their assessment of the functional use of the 
20 
 
18 Exhibit KCE-08, KCE IR-002-044; Exhibit KCE-09, KCE IR 2-045; Exhibit KCE-10, KCE IR 2-069 
19 Proposed Revision 18 to Leaf 247, Central Hudson PSC No. 15 Electric Tariff (submitted July 31, 2023).  
20 Exhibit KCE-11, KCE-01, IR-002 
3747


--- Page 486 ---
 
 
13 
 
assets to which a project is interconnected.21 The Company also states that the point of 
1 
interconnection alone does not dictate a rate assignment, but must be considered along with the 
2 
character of service to determine which rate would be applicable.22  
3 
 
4 
Q. Please describe the Company’s Standby Service rates and who they apply to?  
5 
A. Service Class 14 (“SC 14”) is Central Hudson’s Standby Rate. SC 14 contains the Standby 
6 
rate versions of the Company’s standard rates.23   Standby rates apply to customers who have on-
7 
site generation with a nameplate capacity greater than 15% of maximum potential onsite demand 
8 
provided that the generation technology is not otherwise exempted from Standby rates24 (e.g., 
9 
solar).  The Commission has determined that Standby rates are also applicable to energy storage 
10 
sized at 1 MW of capacity or greater.25  
11 
 
12 
Q. How do Standby rates relate to the Company’s standard rates? 
13 
A. There is a parent-child relationship between the standard service classes and their 
14 
corresponding Standby rate versions since the Standby rate is derived from the otherwise 
15 
applicable standard rate. Standby rates are designed to be revenue-neutral to the parent standard 
16 
rate and therefore their construction relies upon the same revenue requirements and billing 
17 
determinants used to calculate the standard rate.26  
18

30 Id, pp. 88-89, Order No. 5; The revised ACOS filings will also include Buyback Charges. In Central Hudson’s

Q. Please explain why the revenue requirements, cost of service, cost allocations, and rate 
4 
designs for SC 13 and SC 13 Standby are relevant to a bulk storage project that may take service 
5

6 
A. As the Company states, they intend to leverage their updated Standby rates that will be filed 
7

8

9

10 
requirements, cost allocations and rate designs for the SC 13 parent rate have significant 
11 
implications for bulk storage development.35As the analysis in subsequent sections of the 
12 
testimony demonstrates, if the forthcoming WDS tariff imposes similar costs upon bulk storage 
13 
as the current and proposed (July 2022) SC 13 Standby rates, the result will be a severe 
14 
economic barrier to private sector investment in bulk storage in the Company’s service area.

3 
 
4 
Q. What are implications do these base rate charges have for bulk storage operations? 
5 
A. Setting expansive On-Peak hours for bulk storage facilities, which definitionally will be 
6 
responding to wholesale market signals, detrimentally changes price signal bulk storage will 
7 
respond to – for both charging and discharging.38 This expansive peak period, ranging from 7am 
8 
to 11pm on weekdays, results in an economic barrier to a bulk storage facility that might 
9 
otherwise act to charge opportunistically during daytime periods such as times with excess solar 
10 
generation, contrary to the goals of New York State policy regarding battery storage. This is 
11 
particularly troubling for a facility that is following wholesale market signals in accordance with 
12 
proposed New York State policy programs (consider, for example, the ISC, described above). A 
13 
bulk storage operator will have to consider multiple price signals – NYISO's price signal and the 
14 
price signals set in the Central Hudson’s rates (e.g., On-Peak and Super-Peak) when deciding 
15 
how to bid and what services to provide to the wholesale market. 
16 
 
17

duration battery. Retail rider and adjustment factors, as presently constructed, pose several major 
4 
problems, including: (1) a material cost burden that disadvantages them versus other NYISO 
5 
wholesale market participants connected to transmission facilities subject to the Open Access 
6 
Transmission Tariff (“OATT”) (see testimony below regarding rate treatment for FERC-
7 
jurisdictional facilities); and (2) intra-class inequities in cost recovery since bulk storage facilities 
8 
would pay these riders on energy used for charging and then when the energy is subsequently 
9 
discharged back into the system for consumption by others, those other customers would also 
10 
pay the rider charges on the same electrons (see further discussion in Section VII, below). 
11 
3755


--- Page 494 ---
 
 
21 
 
 
1 
Q. Please describe the impact of the base rate charges and the rider and adjustment factors on 
2 
bulk storage project economics.  
3 
A. The impact is very significant. In the example provided above, the monthly base rate charges 
4 
translate to $5.86 per kW-month and $4.89 per kW-month for existing and proposed revised SC 
5 
13 Standby rates, respectively. The costs associated with rider and adjustment factors translate to 
6 
an additional $1.03 per kW-month. In essence, this means that each kW of bulk storage capacity 
7 
would need to generate between approximately $5.92 and $6.89 per kW-month in value in the 
8 
wholesale market each month solely to pay for base service, let alone to generate the revenue 
9 
needed to recoup capital expenditures, cover operating costs, and generate profit.  
10 
 
11 
Q. Please elaborate on the impact of the monthly base rate charges and the rider and adjustment 
12 
factors. 
13 
A. We offer a couple data points to contextualize the significant cost of the up to $6.89 per kW-
14 
month of expected base rate and rider charges to bulk storage in Central Hudson. First, one can 
15 
observe that the NYISO Installed Capacity Strip Auction clearing prices for Zone G have not 
16 
exceeded $5.04 per kW-month over the past five years, and average annual Strip Auction 
17 
clearing prices have not exceeded $6.66 per kW-month since 2015.39 This is before accounting 
18 
for the fact that bulk storage projects will only realize a percentage of such capacity revenues 
19 
because the amount of qualified capacity a facility can sell is less than the facility’s nameplate 
20 
capacity, due to NYISO’s capacity accreditation rules. The fact that base rate and rider charges 
21 
incurred by bulk storage exceed the recent available capacity market revenues – one of the key 
22 
 
39 Exhibit KCE-25, “Strip Auction Summary”, NYISO Installed Capacity, available at 
http://icap.nyiso.com/ucap/public/auc_view_strip_detail.do (last visited Nov. 21, 2023). 
3756


--- Page 495 ---
 
 
22 
 
wholesale market revenue streams on which bulk storage depends (the others being energy 
1 
arbitrage and ancillary service revenues) – is a strong indication of the magnitude of the impact 
2 
and cost of Central Hudson’s proposed rates. 
3 
Second, one can compare the cost of the base rate and rider charges to the overall levelized cost 
4 
of bulk storage. According to Lazard’s Levelized Cost of Storage Analysis – Version 8.0, the 
5 
levelized cost of a 100 MW, 2-hour utility-scale standalone battery storage is between $104 and 
6 
$180 per kW-year (i.e., between $8.7 and $15.0 per kW-month).40 This means that Central 
7 
Hudson’s base rate charges equate to between approximately 33% - 68% of the levelized cost of 
8 
a battery system. 
9 
 
10 
Q. What are the overall implications of the high costs of monthly base rate charges and rider and 
11 
adjustment factors Central Hudson proposes to apply to bulk storage? 
12 
A. As discussed below, an identical bulk storage project interconnected to a FERC-jurisdictional 
13 
transmission line would not have to pay base rate charges and rider and adjustment factors, and 
14 
as a result, would be far more economic. These economics make it extremely difficult, if not 
15 
impossible, to develop bulk storage in Central Hudson’s service territory.  Substantially more in-
16 
state support would be required in Central Hudson’s service territory than in parts of the state 
17 
that are federally-jurisdictional or have more favorable state-jurisdictional rates. 
18 
 
19 
 
40 Exhibit KCE-26, Bilicic, G. & Scroggins, S. (April 2023), “Lazard’s Levelized Cost of Storage Analysis – Version 
8.0” at p.18 available at  Lazard’s LCOE+ Report, available at https://www.lazard.com/media/2ozoovyg/lazards-
lcoeplus-april-2023.pdf (last visited Nov. 20, 2023).

23 
 
VI. An Alternative Ratemaking Framework Would Allow for Reasonable Rates and Permit Bulk 
1 
Storage Investment in Central Hudson Territory   
2 
Q. Are there other ratemaking frameworks that would more properly align battery storage rates 
3 
with New York State’s policy supporting bulk storage development? 
4 
A. Yes. In Order 841, the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission (FERC) recognized that 
5 
storage interconnected to the FERC-jurisdictional transmission system that provides a wholesale 
6 
service may not be charged certain transmission costs. This policy is pivotal in permitting 
7 
storage to compete in wholesale markets and provide the services that New York State has 
8 
concluded are necessary to meet its public policy goals. Based on currently available record 
9 
information, Central Hudson has indicated that all storage interconnected to its system will pay 
10 
state-jurisdictional charges, even when providing a wholesale service. The record indicates that 
11 
this is true for projects interconnected at 69, 115, and potentially 345 kV41. One option is to 
12 
determine that bulk storage interconnected at these high voltages should receive rate treatment 
13 
analogous to the rate treatment that FERC Order 841 accords bulk storage interconnected to 
14 
FERC-jurisdictional facilities.  
15 
 
16 
Q. What does FERC Order 841 say regarding transmission charges? 
17 
A. Just as New York State has recognized the unique beneficial characteristics of storage, in 
18 
Order 841, FERC recognized that storage has “unique physical and operational characteristics -- 
19 
including their ability to both inject energy into the grid and receive energy from it “and that 
20 
storage will help “support the resilience of the bulk power system.”42  FERC further found that 
21 
“electric storage resources that are dispatched to consume electricity to provide a service in the 
22 
 
41   Please see footnote 1.   
42  Order 841, Paragraph 2. 
3758


--- Page 497 ---
 
 
24 
 
RTO/ISO markets (such as frequency regulation or a downward ramping service) should not pay 
1 
the same transmission charges as load during the provision of that service.”43   
2 
 
3 
Q. Please comment on how rate treatment of bulk storage in Central Hudson territory differs 
4 
from rate treatment of bulk storage under FERC Order 841. 
5 
A. At this time it appears that all of Central Hudson’s facilities – including higher-voltage 
6 
facilities – are funded via state-jurisdictional rates. Therefore, bulk storage in Central Hudson’s 
7 
service territory must pay all of Central Hudson’s state jurisdictional charges, including charges 
8 
that recoup the cost of grid infrastructure 69 kV and above.44 The practical effect of this is that a 
9 
bulk storage project in Central Hudson service territory does not experience the beneficial rate 
10 
treatment that Order 841 accords an otherwise identical bulk storage that is interconnected at the 
11 
same voltage but to a FERC-jurisdictional transmission facility.    
12 
 
13 
Q. What investment incentives does Central Hudson’s rate structure send to battery storage 
14 
investors?    
15 
A.   Currently, Central Hudson’s rate structure sends a signal that discourages battery investment 
16 
in its service territory.  Under the current policy structure, a facility interconnected in Central 
17 
Hudson’s service territory is substantially more uneconomic than a facility interconnected to a 
18 
FERC-jurisdictional line of the same size in the same state. Thus, bulk energy storage projects in 
19 
Central Hudson’s territory would be unlikely to win competitive solicitations under the ISC 
20 
mechanism, driving project developers away from Central Hudson’s service territory. This would 
21 
 
43 Order 841, Paragraph 293.   
44 As indicated in footnote 2, in this testimony we take no position on whether Central Hudson’s rate treatment is 
correct pursuant to state or federal law. In this testimony our focus is on the practical ramifications of Central 
Hudson’s rates on the development bulk storage in the Company’s service territory. 
3759


--- Page 498 ---
 
 
25 
 
make it harder to meet state policy goals and limit any local benefits that bulk storage might 
1 
bring to Central Hudson’s service territory. 
2 
 
3 
Q. What options does the Commission have to align Central Hudson’s rates with the policies set 
4 
forth in Order 841?   
5 
A.  Acknowledging the willingness and commitment that Central Hudson has shown to 
6 
proactively work with KCE on these issues and given the substantial number project 
7 
interconnections that Central Hudson has on its 69 kV + system (see Table KCE-1), the best 
8 
option is for Central Hudson to take key elements of Order 841 policy into account in both its 
9 
ACOS rates and WDS tariff. Specifically, bulk storage projects participating in the wholesale 
10 
market should be exempted from paying Central Hudson’s rates when providing a service to the 
11 
NYISO45. Such policy would resolve the current rate treatment discrepancy that exists between 
12 
bulk storage interconnected to higher-voltage facilities in Central Hudson territory and such 
13 
projects interconnected to FERC-jurisdictional transmission facilities elsewhere. 
14 
 
15 
Q. In addition to the federal ratemaking framework offered by FERC Order 841, are there 
16 
existing in-state ratemaking frameworks that more properly align battery storage rates with New 
17 
York State’s policy supporting bulk storage development?  
18 
A. Yes. The distribution tariff for Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid 
19 
(“National Grid”) includes a special provision for wholesale generators who are registered 
20 
customers of NYISO and who receive Station Power under the provisions of the NYISO ISO

Inc. Compliance Filing: Energy Storage Resources, FERC Docket No. ER19-467-000, pp. 2-3 (August 25, 2020).

Market Administration and Control Area Services Tariff.46 Please note that this rate is the 
1

2

Q. Please summarize your conclusions regarding Central Hudson’s current rate treatment and 
1 
your proposed alternative ratemaking framework. 
2 
A. Bulk storage projects will be preferentially constructed where (a) facilities can pay Order 841-
3 
compliant rates (e.g., interconnect to FERC-jurisdictional facilities); and (b) facilities can 
4 
interconnect to state-jurisdictional facilities where rates are more economic for large-scale 
5 
battery deployment.  Incorporating an Order 841-style exemption from base rate charges for bulk 
6 
storage facilities when they are providing a service to the NYISO would resolve the issues posed 
7 
by application of Central Hudson’s current as-proposed rates to all bulk storage in Central 
8 
Hudson’s service territory. 
9 
 
10 
VII. Bulk Storage Should be Exempt from Paying Rider and Adjustment Factors 
11 
Q. Earlier in the testimony there is a reference to riders and adjustment factors. Please describe 
12 
the purpose of rider and adjustment factors. 
13 
A. Riders and adjustment factors are retail charges that recover the costs of specific programs 
14 
and items designated for cost recovery outside of base rates. With the exception of the RDM and 
15 
Rate Adjustment Mechanism (“RAM”) charges, they are independent of base distribution rates.51 
16 
 
17 
Q. What makes bulk storage different from other “customers” on the grid with respect to riders 
18 
and adjustment factors? 
19 
A. A bulk storage project that participates in NYISO markets is not the final point of sale for the 
20 
energy used in charging. With the exception of losses, the energy used to charge a bulk storage 
21 
 
51 RAM Adjustment charges are to reconcile regulatory assets, deferrals, and variances in certain charges relative to 
the provisions in base rates. See New York PSC, Order Adopting Terms of Joint Proposal & Establishing Electric & 
Gas Rate Plan, Case No. 20-E-0428 at p. 22-23 (Nov. 18, 2021). 
3763


--- Page 502 ---
 
 
29 
 
project will be subsequently discharged during a higher value time period for ultimate final 
1 
consumption by other customers connected to the grid. In essence, it is a purchase for resale to 
2 
an eventual retail customer, who will be responsible for paying the rider charges. To charge a 
3 
bulk storage project for the rider costs while it is participating in wholesale markets and then to 
4 
charge the retail customer again is akin to charging twice on the same electrons. This electricity 
5 
in bulk storage systems is not being consumed for retail use, but rather stored by the technology 
6 
waiting to be dispatched back to the system at an economic period. Application of full retail 
7 
riders and other adjustment factor charges are not appropriate to this technology.  
8 
 
9 
Q. Is it appropriate for a WDS tariff to include riders and adjustment factors? 
10 
A. No. Rider and adjustment factors are retail charges that recover the costs of state-
11 
jurisdictional programs such as the System Benefits Charge (“SBC”). The SBC includes costs for 
12 
the following programs: clean energy activities conducted by NYSERDA; costs for the Clean 
13 
Energy Fund, Renewable Portfolio Standard, and Energy Efficiency Portfolio Standard.52  These 
14 
types of retail program charges do not belong in a WDS tariff for assets transacting in FERC 
15 
jurisdictional markets. In addition to jurisdiction issues, these charges pose a severe economic 
16 
disadvantage to bulk storage projects whose business model relies upon time-based participation 
17 
in wholesale markets in competition with other wholesale market participants.  
18 
 
19 
Q. Is there precedent in New York for exempting certain customers from paying riders and 
20 
adjustment factors? 
21 
 
52 Leaf 163.3, Rev 18, Central Hudson Electric Tariff PSC No. 15. 
3764


--- Page 503 ---
 
 
30 
 
A. Yes. Central Hudson has indicated that several categories of customers do not pay certain 
1 
rider surcharges on their bills such as those participating in the Excelsior Jobs Program and 
2 
Recharge New York program.53   
3 
 
4 
Q. In your experience are other utilities considering waiving surcharges like the SBC and other 
5 
riders for bulk storage participating in the wholesale market? 
6 
A. Yes. In Connecticut, the distribution utilities are engaging with a stakeholder working group 
7 
to develop a WDS tariff. While that tariff is still in development, all parties have agreed that state 
8 
jurisdictional retail riders and adjustment factors should not be included in the rates. The 
9 
Connecticut utilities have made filings to the Public Utility Regulatory Authority (“PURA”) with 
10 
draft iterations of the WDS tariff that omit these rider charges.54, 55  
11 
 
12

16 
evenly as six invoices issued to Substation rate option customers and six invoices issued to 
17 
Transmission rate option customers.56 Recent monthly billed kW demands for SC 13 are shown

in the graphic below.

produce the revenues commensurate with the entire existing SC 13 customer group and in excess

of the revenue requirement for SC 13 Transmission suggests that the current SC 13 rate design is

2 
queue.

Q. Does assignment of bulk storage to the SC 13 Standby rate raise concerns about how revenue 
5 
decoupling will work due to the small size of the rate class? 
6

13 have been raised in prior dockets due to the limited number of customers on the rate,62 and

the October 2023 Order in Case 15-E-0751. Central Hudson’s July 2022 compliance filing

13 
of the Company’s filing.63

16 
A. In the Accounting and Tax panel in this docket, the Company’s witnesses stated that “the 
17 
Company plans to file a Wholesale Distribution Service tariff with FERC to appropriately charge

Q. What can you conclude about the risk of rate instability for SC 13 if bulk storage projects are

assigned to that rate?  
13 
A. The risk of volatility in SC 13 has implications for other customers on the rate and developers 
14 
and investors in bulk storage projects. Bulk storage projects require predictable non-market 
15 
operating costs the risk of a volatile reset in the distribution rate is at odds with the certainty that 
16 
investors require to commit capital to projects. As a result, there is an urgent need for a WDS 
17 
tariff that is structured in a way to provide stable and predictable long-term costs. 
18 
 
19 
IX. Conclusions 
20 
Q. Why should the Commission take action in this case as opposed to a more general docket 
21 
focused on bulk storage? 
22 
 
64 Central Hudson, Accounting and Tax Panel, at 36:16-22. 
3769


--- Page 508 ---
 
 
35 
 
A. First, the Commission recognized in the ACOS proceeding that individual utility rates would 
1 
be considered in individual rate cases, in recognition of the very detailed and unique 
2 
circumstances for each utility.65 Failure to consider Central Hudson’s rates in this docket, and to 
3 
address the structural issues highlighted here would result in further uncertainty for developers as 
4 
the state seeks to meet its renewable energy and storage goals. This problem is exacerbated in 
5 
Central Hudson’s service territory, where the state-jurisdictional rate is applied to bulk storage 
6 
interconnected at 69, 115, and, potentially, 345kV.66 In addition, as discussed above, Central 
7 
Hudson’s rates for bulk storage have implications for this broader rate case, including risks of 
8 
overcollection and rate instability. The Commission can promote a more efficient and equitable 
9 
energy system that meets the state policy goals by requiring rates that recognize the structure of 
10 
the Central Hudson service territory and the benefits that bulk storage brings to the grid. That 
11 
requires further recognition that if the forthcoming WDS tariff imposes similar costs upon bulk 
12 
storage as the current and proposed (July 2022) SC 13 Standby rates, the result will be a severe 
13 
economic barrier to private sector investment in bulk storage throughout the Company’s service 
14 
area. Notably, this barrier to investment in bulk storage appears to extend to all high voltages 
15 
within Central Hudson’s territory, voltages that the state anticipates will host the larger bulk 
16 
storage facilities that participate in the wholesale market.   
17 
Central Hudson has indicated that it will take state-jurisdictional rates into account in its proposal 
18 
for its WDS submission. Especially given the higher voltage battery storage interconnections that 
19 
are included in Central Hudson’s distribution service territory, it makes sense to consider the 
20 
policies set forth in Order 841 in establishing the WDS tariff. Furthermore, establishing an 
21 
 
65 “Order Establishing Updated Standby Service Rates and Implementing Optional Mass Market Demand Rates”, 
Case 15-E-0751, p. 46 (October 13, 2023). 
66 See footnotes 1 and 2 above. 
3770


--- Page 509 ---
 
 
36 
 
exemption from base rate charges – as FERC did in Order 841 for transmission rates – has some 
1 
level of precedent in other New York distribution service territories, for example, in National 
2 
Grid SC7 – SC3A, 60+ kV (Special Provision J).  WDS tariffs have limited precedent and the 
3 
standards that FERC may apply are uncertain. Finally, as noted in the testimony, riders and 
4 
adjustment factors should be appropriately excluded from a FERC-jurisdictional WDS tariff. 
5 
We acknowledge Central Hudson’s consideration of how to approach the complex issues raised 
6 
in this testimony as it contemplates its WDS and ACOS filings and appreciate the Commission’s 
7 
consideration of how important these issues are for rate equity and to meet ratepayer needs. Swift 
8 
resolution of these concerns would limit uncertainty and permit battery storage developers to 
9 
commit the substantial investments required to get battery storage built in Central Hudson’s 
10 
service area.  
11 
 
12 
Q. Does this conclude your testimony? 
13 
A. Yes, it does. 
14
